**Scotland** ([Scots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_language): *Scotland*; [Scottish Gaelic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic_language): *[Alba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alba)*) is a [country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countries_of_the_United_Kingdom) that is part of the [United Kingdom](31717.md), consisting of the northern part of the island of [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain) and adjacent [islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islands_of_Scotland), principally in the archipelagos of the [Hebrides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrides) and the [Northern Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Isles). In 2022 the country's population was about 5.4 million.[^cite_note-ONS_Geography_Guide-17] Its capital city is [Edinburgh](9602.md), whilst [Glasgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasgow) is the largest and the most populous of the [cities in the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cities_of_Scotland). Scotland [borders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Scottish_border) [England](9316.md) to the south, the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md) to the north and west, the [North Sea](21179.md) to the east, and the [Irish Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Sea) to the south-west. In the [Scottish Parliament](61188.md), 129 [members](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_the_Scottish_Parliament) represent 73 [constituencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Scottish_Parliament_constituencies_and_electoral_regions_\(2026\)).[^cite_note-18] The [Scottish Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Government) is the [executive arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_\(government\)) of the devolved government, headed by the [first minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Minister_of_Scotland), who chairs the [cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Cabinet) and is responsible for government policy and [international engagement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_relations_of_Scotland).[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20]

The [Kingdom of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland) emerged as a [sovereign state](23604120.md) in the 9th century. [Independence from England was maintained](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wars_of_Scottish_Independence) partly through an [alliance with France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auld_Alliance). In 1603, [James VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_VI) succeeded to the thrones of [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England) and [Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Ireland), forming a [personal union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_union) of the [three kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_the_Crowns). On 1 May 1707, Scotland and England combined to create the new [Kingdom of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain),[^cite_note-Keay-21][^cite_note-Mackie-22] with the [Parliament of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Scotland) subsumed into the [Parliament of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Great_Britain). In 1999, a [Scottish Parliament](61188.md) was re-established, and has [devolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution) authority over many areas of [domestic policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_policy).[^cite_note-23] The country has its own distinct [legal system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_law), [education system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Scotland) and [religious history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Christianity_in_Scotland), which have all contributed to the continuation of [Scottish society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_society), [Scottish culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Scotland) and [national identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_national_identity).[^cite_note-administrative_control-24] [Scottish English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_English) and [Scots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_language) are the most widely spoken [languages in the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Scotland), existing on a [dialect continuum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dialect_continuum) with each other.[^cite_note-25] [Scottish Gaelic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic) speakers can be found all over Scotland, but the language is largely spoken natively by communities within the [Hebrides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrides);[^cite_note-26] Gaelic speakers now constitute less than 2% of the total population, although state-sponsored [revitalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revitalisation_of_Scottish_Gaelic) attempts have led to a growing community of [second language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_language) speakers.[^cite_note-27]

&#x20;The mainland of Scotland is broadly divided into three regions: the [Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Highlands), a mountainous region in the north and north-west; the [Lowlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Lowlands), a flatter plain across the centre of the country; and the [Southern Uplands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Uplands), a hilly region along the southern border. The Highlands are the most mountainous region of the British Isles and contain its highest peak, [Ben Nevis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Nevis), at 4,413 feet (1,345 m).[^cite_note-ONS_Geography_Guide-17] The region also contains many lakes, called [lochs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loch); the term is also applied to the many saltwater inlets along the country's deeply indented western coastline. The geography of the many islands is varied. Some, such as [Mull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Mull) and [Skye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Skye), are noted for their mountainous terrain, while [Tiree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiree) and [Coll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coll) are flatter.

## Etymology

Main article: [Etymology of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etymology_of_Scotland)

*Scotland* comes from *[Scoti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scoti)*, the Latin name for the [Gaels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaels).[^cite_note-28] [Philip Freeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_Freeman) has speculated on the likelihood of a group of raiders adopting a name from an [Indo-European root](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_root), \**skot*, citing the parallel in Greek *skotos* (σκότος), meaning 'darkness, gloom'.[^cite_note-29] The [Late Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Latin) word *[Scotia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotia)* ("land of the Gaels") was initially used to refer to Ireland,[^cite_note-30] and likewise in early [Old English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_English_language) *Scotland* was used for Ireland.[^cite_note-31] By the 11th century at the latest, *Scotia* was being used to refer to (Gaelic-speaking) Scotland north of the [River Forth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Forth), alongside *Albania* or *Albany*, both derived from the Gaelic *[Alba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alba)*.[^cite_note-Brewer-32] The use of the words *Scots* and *Scotland* to encompass all of what is now Scotland became common in the [Late Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_in_the_Late_Middle_Ages).[^cite_note-Keay-21]

## History

Main article: [History of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Scotland)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Scottish history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Scottish_history).

### Prehistory

The earliest known evidence of human presence in Scotland is [Hamburgian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamburgian_culture) stone tools produced by late [Upper Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Paleolithic) [hunter gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer) who arrived in Scotland during the [Bølling–Allerød Interstadial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%B8lling%E2%80%93Aller%C3%B8d_Interstadial) warm period at the end of the [last ice age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_ice_age), around 14,500 to 14,000 years ago, shortly following the retreat of the ice sheet that had previously covered Scotland.[^cite_note-33][^cite_note-34] [Neolithic farmers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_European_Farmers) arrived in Scotland around 6000 years ago.[^cite_note-35] The well-preserved village of [Skara Brae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skara_Brae) on the mainland of [Orkney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orkney) dates from this period. Neolithic habitation, burial, and ritual sites are particularly common and well preserved in the [Northern Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Isles) and [Western Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Isles), where a lack of trees led to most structures being built of local stone.[^cite_note-36] Evidence of sophisticated pre-Christian belief systems is demonstrated by sites such as the [Callanish Stones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Callanish_Stones) on [Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Lewis), and the [Maes Howe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maes_Howe) on Orkney, which were built in the third millennium BC.[^cite_note-short-37]<sup>: 38 </sup>

### Early history

Main articles: [Scotland during the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_during_the_Roman_Empire), [Roman Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Britain), and [Pictland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pictland)



[Skara Brae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skara_Brae), Europe's most complete [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) village, occupied from roughly 3180 BC – 2500 BC

The first written reference to Scotland was in 320 BC by Greek sailor [Pytheas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pytheas), who called the northern tip of Britain "Orcas", the source of the name of the Orkney islands.[^cite_note-Forsyth-38]<sup>: 10 </sup>

Most of modern Scotland was not [incorporated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_conquest_of_Britain) into the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), and Roman control over parts of the area fluctuated over a rather short period. The first Roman incursion into Scotland was in 79 AD, when [Agricola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gnaeus_Julius_Agricola) invaded Scotland; he defeated a Caledonian army at the [Battle of Mons Graupius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Mons_Graupius) in 83 AD.[^cite_note-Forsyth-38]<sup>: 12 </sup> After the Roman victory, Roman forts were briefly set along the [Gask Ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gask_Ridge) close to the [Highland line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_Boundary_Fault), but by three years after the battle, the Roman armies had withdrawn to the [Southern Uplands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Uplands).[^cite_note-39] Remains of Roman forts established in the 1st century have been found as far north as the [Moray Firth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moray_Firth).[^cite_note-\:4-40] By the reign of the [Roman emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_emperor) [Trajan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trajan) (r. 98–117), Roman control had lapsed to Britain south of a line between the [River Tyne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Tyne) and the [Solway Firth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solway_Firth).[^cite_note-\:8-41] Along this line, Trajan's successor [Hadrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrian) (r. 117–138) erected [Hadrian's Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrian's_Wall) in northern England[^cite_note-Forsyth-38]<sup>: 12 </sup> and the *[Limes Britannicus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limes_Britannicus)* became the northern border of the Roman Empire.[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43] The Roman influence on the southern part of the country was considerable, and they introduced [Christianity](5211.md) to Scotland.[^cite_note-Forsyth-38]<sup>: 13–14 </sup>[^cite_note-short-37]<sup>: 38 </sup>

The [Antonine Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonine_Wall) was built from 142 at the order of Hadrian's successor [Antoninus Pius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoninus_Pius) (r. 138–161), defending the Roman part of Scotland from the unadministered part of the island, north of a line between the [Firth of Clyde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firth_of_Clyde) and the [Firth of Forth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firth_of_Forth).[^cite_note-44] The [Roman invasion of Caledonia 208–210](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_invasion_of_Caledonia_208%E2%80%93210) was undertaken by emperors of the imperial [Severan dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severan_dynasty) in response to the breaking of a treaty by the Caledonians in 197,[^cite_note-\:4-40] but permanent conquest of the whole of Great Britain was forestalled by Roman forces becoming bogged down in punishing [guerrilla warfare](12720.md) and the death of the senior emperor [Septimius Severus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Septimius_Severus) (r. 193–211) at [Eboracum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eboracum) ([York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/York)) after he was taken ill while on campaign. Although forts erected by the [Roman army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_army) in the Severan campaign were placed near those established by Agricola and were clustered at the mouths of the [glens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glens) in the Highlands, the Caledonians were again in revolt in 210–211 and these were overrun.[^cite_note-\:4-40]



[Callanish Stones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Callanish_Stones), erected in the late [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) era

To the Roman historians [Tacitus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacitus) and [Cassius Dio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassius_Dio), the [Scottish Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Highlands) and the area north of the [River Forth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Forth) was called [Caledonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caledonia).[^cite_note-\:4-40] According to Cassius Dio, the inhabitants of Caledonia were the [Caledonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caledonians) and the [Maeatae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maeatae).[^cite_note-\:4-40] Other ancient authors used the adjective "Caledonian" to mean anywhere in northern or inland Britain, often mentioning the region's people and animals, its cold climate, its [pearls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pearl), and a noteworthy region of wooded hills ([Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language): *saltus*) which the 2nd century AD Roman philosopher [Ptolemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemy), in his [*Geography*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_\(Ptolemy\)), described as being south-west of the [Beauly Firth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beauly_Firth).[^cite_note-\:4-40] The name Caledonia is echoed in the place names of [Dunkeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunkeld), [Rohallion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohallion), and [Schiehallion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schiehallion).[^cite_note-\:4-40]

The [Great Conspiracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Conspiracy) constituted a seemingly coordinated invasion against Roman rule in Britain in the later 4th century, which included the participation of the Gaelic [Scoti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scoti) and the Caledonians, who were then known as [Picts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picts) by the Romans. This was defeated by the [*comes* Theodosius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comes_Theodosius); but Roman military government was withdrawn from the island altogether by the early 5th century, resulting in the [Anglo-Saxon settlement of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_settlement_of_Britain) and the immigration of the [Saxons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saxons) to southeastern Scotland and the rest of eastern Great Britain.[^cite_note-\:8-41]

### Kingdom of Scotland

Main articles: [Kingdom of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland), [Scotland in the Early Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_in_the_Early_Middle_Ages), [Scotland in the High Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_in_the_High_Middle_Ages), and [Scotland in the Late Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_in_the_Late_Middle_Ages)



Political divisions in early medieval Scotland



Norse kingdoms at the end of the eleventh century

Beginning in the sixth century, the area that is now Scotland was divided into four areas: [Pictland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pictland), a patchwork of small lordships in central Scotland;[^cite_note-Forsyth-38]<sup>: 25–26 </sup> the [Anglo-Saxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Saxon) [Kingdom of Northumbria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Northumbria), which had conquered southeastern Scotland;[^cite_note-Forsyth-38]<sup>: 18–20 </sup> Northern Brittonic territory likely centred on [Alt Clut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alt_Clut) (Dumbarton Rock) and the Clyde valley;[^cite_note-45] and [Dál Riata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A1l_Riata), which included territory in western Scotland and northern Ireland, and spread Gaelic language and culture into Scotland.[^cite_note-46] These societies were based on the family unit and had sharp divisions in wealth, although the vast majority were poor and worked full-time in [subsistence agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsistence_agriculture). The Picts kept slaves (mostly captured in war) through the ninth century.[^cite_note-Forsyth-38]<sup>: 26–27 </sup>

Gaelic influence over Pictland and Northumbria was facilitated by the large number of [Gaelic-speaking clerics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_Church) working as missionaries.[^cite_note-Forsyth-38]<sup>: 23–24 </sup> Operating in the sixth century on the island of [Iona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iona), [Saint Columba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Columba) was one of the earliest and best-known missionaries.[^cite_note-short-37]<sup>: 39 </sup> The [Vikings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vikings) began to raid Scotland in the eighth century. Although the raiders sought slaves and luxury items, their main motivation was to acquire land. The oldest [Norse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norse_Scotland) settlements were in northwest Scotland, but they eventually conquered many areas along the coast. [Old Norse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Norse) entirely displaced [Pictish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pictish_language) in the [Northern Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Isles).[^cite_note-47]

In the ninth century, the Norse threat allowed a Gael named [Kenneth I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenneth_I) (Cináed mac Ailpín) to seize power over Pictland, establishing a royal dynasty to which the modern monarchs trace their lineage, and marking the beginning of the end of Pictish culture.[^cite_note-Forsyth-38]<sup>: 31–32 </sup>[^cite_note-48] The kingdom of Cináed and his descendants, called Alba, was Gaelic in character but existed on the same area as Pictland. By the end of the tenth century, the Pictish language went extinct as its speakers shifted to Gaelic.[^cite_note-Forsyth-38]<sup>: 32–33 </sup> From a base in eastern Scotland north of the [River Forth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Forth) and south of the [River Spey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Spey), the kingdom expanded first southwards, into the former Northumbrian lands, and northwards into [Moray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Moray).[^cite_note-Forsyth-38]<sup>: 34–35 </sup> Around the turn of the millennium, there was a centralization in agricultural lands and the first towns began to be established.[^cite_note-Forsyth-38]<sup>: 36–37 </sup>



[James V of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_V_of_Scotland) at the [Court of Session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Session) in 1532, at [Parliament House, Edinburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_House,_Edinburgh), the [Parliament of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Scotland) until 1707

In the twelfth and thirteenth centuries, much of Scotland was under the control of a single ruler. Initially, Gaelic culture predominated, but immigrants from France, England and Flanders steadily created a more diverse society, with the Gaelic language starting to be replaced by Scots; and a modern nation-state emerged from this. At the end of this period, war against England started the growth of a [Scottish national consciousness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_national_consciousness).[^cite_note-Stringer-49][^cite_note-Barrell_2000-50]<sup>: ch 1 </sup> [David I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_I_of_Scotland) (1124–1153) and his successors centralised royal power[^cite_note-Stringer-49]<sup>: 41–42 </sup> and united mainland Scotland, capturing regions such as Moray, [Galloway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galloway), and [Caithness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caithness), although he could not extend his power over the [Hebrides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrides), which had been ruled by various [Scottish clans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_clans) following the death of [Somerled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somerled) in 1164.[^cite_note-Stringer-49]<sup>: 48–49 </sup> In 1266, Scotland fought the short but consequential [Scottish-Norwegian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish-Norwegian_War) which saw the reclamation of the Hebrides after the strong defeat of King [Haakon IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haakon_IV) and his forces at the [Battle of Largs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Largs).[^cite_note-51] Up until that point, the Hebrides had been under Norwegian Viking control for roughly 400 years and had developed a distinctive [Norse–Gaelic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norse%E2%80%93Gaelic) culture that saw many [Old Norse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Norse) loanwords enter the [Scottish Gaelic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic) spoken by islanders, and through successive generations the Norse would become almost completely assimilated into [Gaelic culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaelic_culture) and the [Scottish clan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_clan) system. After the conflict, Scotland had to affirm Norwegian sovereignty of the [Northern Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Isles), but they were later integrated into Scotland in the 15th century. Scandinavian culture in the form of the [Norn language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norn_language) survived for a lot longer than in the Hebrides, and would strongly influence the local [Scots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_language) dialect on [Shetland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shetland) and [Orkney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orkney).[^cite_note-52] Later, a system of [feudalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feudalism) was consolidated, with both Anglo-Norman incomers and native Gaelic chieftains being granted land in exchange for serving the king.[^cite_note-Stringer-49]<sup>: 53–54 </sup> The relationship with [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England) was complex during this period: Scottish kings tried several times, sometimes with success, to exploit English political turmoil, followed by the longest period of peace between Scotland and England in the mediaeval period: from 1217–1296.[^cite_note-Stringer-49]<sup>: 45-46 </sup>

### Wars of Scottish Independence

Main articles: [Wars of Scottish Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wars_of_Scottish_Independence), [First War of Scottish Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_War_of_Scottish_Independence), and [Second War of Scottish Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_War_of_Scottish_Independence)



Depiction of the [Battle of Stirling Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Stirling_Bridge), fought during the [First War of Scottish Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_War_of_Scottish_Independence), 1297

The death of [Alexander III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_III_of_Scotland) in March 1286 broke the succession line of Scotland's kings. [Edward I of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_I_of_England) arbitrated between various claimants for the Scottish crown. In return for surrendering Scotland's nominal independence, [John Balliol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Balliol) was pronounced king in 1292.[^cite_note-Stringer-49]<sup>: 47 </sup>[^cite_note-Scotland_Conquered-53] In 1294, Balliol and other Scottish lords refused Edward's demands to serve in his army against the French. Scotland and France sealed a treaty on 23 October 1295, known as the [Auld Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auld_Alliance). War ensued, and John was deposed by Edward who took personal control of Scotland. [Andrew Moray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Moray) and [William Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Wallace) initially emerged as the principal leaders of the resistance to English rule in the [Wars of Scottish Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wars_of_Scottish_Independence),[^cite_note-54] until [Robert the Bruce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_the_Bruce) was crowned king of Scotland in 1306.[^cite_note-55] Victory at the [Battle of Bannockburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bannockburn) in 1314 proved the Scots had regained control of their kingdom. In 1320 the world's first documented declaration of independence, the [Declaration of Arbroath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Arbroath), won the support of [Pope John XXII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_John_XXII), leading to the legal recognition of Scottish sovereignty by the English Crown.[^cite_note-Brown-56]<sup>: 70, 72 </sup>

A civil war between the [Bruce dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_dynasty) and their long-term rivals of the [House of Comyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Comyn) and [House of Balliol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Balliol) lasted until the middle of the 14th century. Although the Bruce faction was successful, [David II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_II_of_Scotland)'s lack of an heir allowed his half-nephew [Robert II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_II_of_Scotland), the [Lord High Steward of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_High_Steward_of_Scotland), to come to the throne and establish the [House of Stewart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Stewart).[^cite_note-Brown-56]<sup>: 77 </sup> The Stewarts ruled Scotland for the remainder of the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages). The country they ruled experienced greater prosperity from the end of the 14th century through the Scottish Renaissance to the [Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Reformation),[^cite_note-Mason-57]<sup>: 93 </sup> despite the effects of the [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death) in 1349[^cite_note-Brown-56]<sup>: 76 </sup> and increasing division between [Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Highlands) and [Lowlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Lowlands).[^cite_note-Brown-56]<sup>: 78 </sup> Multiple truces reduced warfare on the southern border.[^cite_note-Brown-56]<sup>: 76, 83 </sup>

### Union of the Crowns

Main articles: [Scotland in the early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_in_the_early_modern_period) and [Union of the Crowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_the_Crowns)



[James VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_VI_and_I), [King of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Scotland), succeeded to the English and Irish thrones in 1603.

The [Treaty of Perpetual Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Perpetual_Peace) was signed in 1502 by [James IV of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_IV_of_Scotland) and [Henry VII of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_VII_of_England). James married Henry's daughter, [Margaret Tudor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Tudor).[^cite_note-58] James invaded England in support of France under the terms of the [Auld Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auld_Alliance) and became the last monarch in [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain) to die in battle, at [Flodden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Flodden) in 1513.[^cite_note-59] The war with England during the minority years of [Mary, Queen of Scots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary,_Queen_of_Scots) between 1543 and 1551 is known as the [Rough Wooing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rough_Wooing).[^cite_note-60] In 1560, the [Treaty of Edinburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Edinburgh) brought an end to the [Siege of Leith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Leith) and recognized the Protestant [Elizabeth I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_I) as Queen of England.[^cite_note-Mason-57]<sup>: 112 </sup> The [Parliament of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Reformation_Parliament) met and immediately adopted the [Scots Confession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_Confession), which signalled the [Scottish Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Reformation)'s sharp break from papal authority and Roman Catholic teaching.[^cite_note-short-37]<sup>: 44 </sup> The Catholic Mary, Queen of Scots, was forced to [abdicate in 1567](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_Anent_the_demission_of_the_Crown_in_favour_of_our_Sovereign_Lord,_and_his_Majesty's_Coronation_1567).[^cite_note-61]

In 1603 [James VI, King of Scots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_VI_and_I) inherited the thrones of the [Kingdom of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England) and the [Kingdom of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Ireland) in the [Union of the Crowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_the_Crowns), and moved to London.[^cite_note-62] This was a [personal union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_union) as despite having the same monarch the kingdoms retained their separate parliaments, laws and other institutions. The first [Union Jack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Jack) was designed at James's behest, to be flown in addition to the [St Andrew's Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Scotland) on Scots vessels at sea. James VI and I intended to create a single kingdom of Great Britain, but was thwarted in his attempt to do so by the [Parliament of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_England), which supported the wrecking proposal that a full legal union be sought instead, a proposal to which the Scots Parliament would not assent, causing the king to withdraw the plan.[^cite_note-63]

Except for a short period under [the Protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Protectorate), Scotland remained a separate state in the 17th century, but there was considerable conflict between the crown and the [Covenanters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covenanters) over the form of [church government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterian_church_governance).[^cite_note-Wormald-64]<sup>: 124 </sup> The military was strengthened, allowing the imposition of royal authority on the western Highland clans. The 1609 [Statutes of Iona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statutes_of_Iona) compelled the cultural integration of Hebridean clan leaders.[^cite_note-65]<sup>: 37–40 </sup> In 1641 and again in 1643, the Parliament of Scotland unsuccessfully sought a union with England which was "federative" and not "incorporating", in which Scotland would retain a separate parliament.[^cite_note-\:5-66] The issue of union split the parliament in 1648.[^cite_note-\:5-66]

After the execution of the Scottish king at [Whitehall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitehall) in 1649, amid the [Wars of the Three Kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wars_of_the_Three_Kingdoms) and [its events in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_in_the_Wars_of_the_Three_Kingdoms), [Oliver Cromwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Cromwell), the victorious [Lord Protector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Protector), imposed the British Isles' first written constitution – the [Instrument of Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrument_of_Government) – on Scotland in 1652 as part of the republican [Commonwealth of England, Scotland, and Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_England,_Scotland,_and_Ireland).[^cite_note-\:5-66] The Protectorate Parliament was the first Westminster parliament to include representatives nominally from Scotland. The monarchy of the [House of Stuart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Stuart) was resumed with the [Restoration in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restoration_in_Scotland) in 1660. The Parliament of Scotland sought a commercial union with England in 1664; the proposal was rejected in 1668.[^cite_note-\:5-66] In 1670 the Parliament of England rejected a proposed political union with Scotland.[^cite_note-\:5-66] English proposals along the same lines were abandoned in 1674 and in 1685.[^cite_note-\:5-66] The Scots Parliament rejected proposals for a political union with England in 1689.[^cite_note-\:5-66] [Jacobitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobitism), the political support for the exiled Catholic Stuart dynasty, remained a threat to the security of the British state under the Protestant [House of Orange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Orange) and the succeeding [House of Hanover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Hanover) until the defeat of the [Jacobite rising of 1745](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobite_rising_of_1745).[^cite_note-\:5-66] In 1698, the [Company of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_of_Scotland) attempted a project to secure a trading colony on the [Isthmus of Panama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isthmus_of_Panama). Almost every Scottish landowner who had money to spare is said to have invested in the [Darien scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darien_scheme).[^cite_note-\:6-67][^cite_note-\:7-68]

### Treaty of Union

Main article: [Treaty of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Union)

Further information: [Kingdom of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain)



Scottish Exemplification (official copy) of the Treaty of Union of 1707

After another proposal from the English House of Lords was rejected in 1695, and a further Lords motion was voted down in the House of Commons in 1700, the Parliament of Scotland again rejected union in 1702.[^cite_note-\:5-66] The failure of the Darien Scheme bankrupted the landowners who had invested, though not the burghs. Nevertheless, the nobles' bankruptcy, along with the threat of an English invasion, played a leading role in convincing the Scots elite to back a union with England.[^cite_note-\:6-67][^cite_note-\:7-68] On 22 July 1706, the [Treaty of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Union) was agreed between representatives of the [Scots Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Scotland) and the [Parliament of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_England). The following year, twin [Acts of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Union_1707) were passed by both parliaments to create the united [Kingdom of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain) with effect from 1 May 1707[^cite_note-69] with popular opposition and anti-union riots in [Edinburgh](9602.md), [Glasgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasgow), and elsewhere.[^cite_note-1707_protests-70][^cite_note-71] The union also created the [Parliament of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Great_Britain), which succeeded both the Parliament of Scotland and the Parliament of England, which rejected proposals from the [Parliament of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Ireland) that the third kingdom be incorporated in the union.[^cite_note-\:5-66]



[James Ogilvy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Ogilvy,_4th_Earl_of_Findlater), former [Lord Chancellor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Chancellor_of_Scotland) (1702–1708), initially supported union but by 1713 advocated for its reversal

[Andrew Fletcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Fletcher_\(patriot\)), a prominent Scottish patriot, argued that the ratification of the treaty would see Scotland "more like a conquered province",[^cite_note-auto3-72] and by 1713, the former [Lord Chancellor of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Chancellor_of_Scotland), [James Ogilvy, 4th Earl of Findlater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Ogilvy,_4th_Earl_of_Findlater), who was a prominent supporter for the Treaty of Union between Scotland and England had changed his position on the treaty, and unsuccessfully advocated for the treaty to be reversed.[^cite_note-73] The deposed [Jacobite Stuart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobitism) claimants had remained popular in the Highlands and north-east, particularly among non-[Presbyterians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterian), including Roman Catholics and [Episcopalian Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopalian_Protestants). Two major Jacobite risings launched in [1715](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobite_rising_of_1715) and [1745](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobite_rising_of_1745) failed to remove the [House of Hanover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Hanover) from the British throne. The threat of the Jacobite movement to the United Kingdom and its monarchs effectively ended at the [Battle of Culloden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Culloden), Great Britain's last [pitched battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pitched_battle).

The passing of the Treaty of Union did not bring about immediate economic prosperity to Scotland as was widely speculated by the [pamphleteer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamphleteer) as a result of the little consideration given to prospects of the Scottish economy.[^cite_note-74] Campaigners for the union between Scotland and England believed that there would be economic advantages to Scotland as a result of the failed [Darien scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darien_scheme) which left the Kingdom of Scotland bankrupt.[^cite_note-auto3-72] Eventually however, with trade tariffs with England abolished, trade blossomed, especially with [Colonial America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_America). The clippers belonging to the Glasgow [Tobacco Lords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tobacco_Lords) were the fastest ships on the route to [Virginia](32432.md). Until the [American War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_War_of_Independence) in 1776, Glasgow was the world's premier tobacco port, dominating world trade.[^cite_note-75] The disparity between the wealth of the merchant classes of the Scottish Lowlands and the ancient clans of the Scottish Highlands grew, amplifying centuries of division.

In the Highlands, clan chiefs gradually started to think of themselves more as commercial landlords than leaders of their people. These social and economic changes included the first phase of the [Highland Clearances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_Clearances) and, ultimately, the demise of clanship.[^cite_note-Devine_1994-76]<sup>: 32–53, *passim* </sup>

### Industrial age and the Scottish Enlightenment

Main article: [Scotland in the modern era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_in_the_modern_era)



[Walter Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Scott), whose [Waverley Novels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waverley_Novels) helped define Scottish identity in the 19th century

The [Scottish Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Enlightenment) and the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) turned Scotland into an intellectual, commercial and industrial powerhouse[^cite_note-77] — so much so [Voltaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voltaire) said "We look to Scotland for all our ideas of civilisation."[^cite_note-78] With the demise of Jacobitism and the advent of the Union, thousands of Scots, mainly Lowlanders, took up numerous positions of power in politics, civil service, the army and navy, trade, economics, colonial enterprises and other areas across the nascent [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire). Historian Neil Davidson notes "after 1746 there was an entirely new level of participation by Scots in political life, particularly outside Scotland." Davidson also states "far from being 'peripheral' to the British economy, Scotland – or more precisely, the Lowlands – lay at its core."[^cite_note-79]

The [Scottish Reform Act 1832](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Reform_Act_1832) increased the number of Scottish MPs and widened the franchise to include more of the middle classes.[^cite_note-Devine\&Finlay1996pp64-5-80] From the mid-century, there were increasing calls for Home Rule for Scotland and the post of [Secretary of State for Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Scotland) was revived.[^cite_note-81] Towards the end of the century prime ministers of Scottish descent included [William Gladstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Ewart_Gladstone),[^cite_note-Quinault2007-82] and [the Earl of Rosebery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archibald_Primrose,_5th_Earl_of_Rosebery).[^cite_note-83] In the late 19th century the growing importance of the working classes was marked by [Keir Hardie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keir_Hardie)'s success in the [Mid Lanarkshire by-election, 1888](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid_Lanarkshire_by-election,_1888), leading to the foundation of the [Scottish Labour Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Labour_Party_\(1888\)), which was absorbed into the [Independent Labour Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Labour_Party) in 1895, with Hardie as its first leader.[^cite_note-84] Glasgow became one of the largest cities in the world and known as "the [Second City of the Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_City_of_the_Empire)" after London.[^cite_note-85] After 1860 the Clydeside shipyards specialised in steamships made of iron (after 1870, made of steel), which rapidly replaced the wooden sailing vessels of both the merchant fleets and the battle fleets of the world. It became the world's pre-eminent shipbuilding centre.[^cite_note-Shields1949-86] The industrial developments, while they brought work and wealth, were so rapid that housing, town planning, and provision for public health did not keep pace with them, and for a time living conditions in some of the towns and cities were notoriously bad, with overcrowding, high infant mortality, and growing rates of tuberculosis.[^cite_note-87]

While the Scottish Enlightenment is traditionally considered to have concluded toward the end of the 18th century,[^cite_note-Magnusson-88] disproportionately large Scottish contributions to British science and letters continued for another 50 years or more, thanks to such figures as the physicists [James Clerk Maxwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Clerk_Maxwell) and [Lord Kelvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Thomson,_1st_Baron_Kelvin), and the engineers and inventors [James Watt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Watt) and [William Murdoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Murdoch), whose work was critical to the technological developments of the Industrial Revolution throughout Britain.[^cite_note-89] In literature, the most successful figure of the mid-19th century was [Walter Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Scott). His first prose work, *[Waverley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waverley_\(novel\))* in 1814, is often called the first historical novel.[^cite_note-90] It launched a highly successful career that probably more than any other helped define and popularise Scottish cultural identity.[^cite_note-91] In the late 19th century, a number of Scottish-born authors achieved international reputations, such as [Robert Louis Stevenson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Louis_Stevenson), [Arthur Conan Doyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Conan_Doyle), [J. M. Barrie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._M._Barrie) and [George MacDonald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_MacDonald).[^cite_note-Cultural_Profile-92] Scotland also played a major part in the development of art and architecture. The [Glasgow School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasgow_School), which developed in the late 19th century, and flourished in the early 20th century, produced a distinctive blend of influences including the [Celtic Revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_Revival) the [Arts and Crafts movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arts_and_Crafts_movement), and [Japonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japonism), which found favour throughout the [modern art](163543.md) world of continental Europe and helped define the [Art Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau) style. Proponents included architect and artist [Charles Rennie Mackintosh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Rennie_Mackintosh).[^cite_note-93]

### World wars and Scotland Act 1998



A piper of the [Seaforth Highlanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seaforth_Highlanders) leads the [26th Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/26th_Infantry_Brigade_\(United_Kingdom\)) back from the trenches during the [Battle of Bazentin Ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bazentin_Ridge), July 1916

Scotland played a major role in the [British effort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_United_Kingdom_during_the_First_World_War) in the [First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World_War). It especially provided manpower, ships, machinery, fish and money.[^cite_note-94] With a population of 4.8 million in 1911, Scotland sent over half a million men to the war, of whom over a quarter died in combat or from disease, and 150,000 were seriously wounded.[^cite_note-95] [Field Marshal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_Marshal_\(United_Kingdom\)) [Sir Douglas Haig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Haig) was Britain's commander on the Western Front. The war saw the emergence of a radical movement called "[Red Clydeside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Clydeside)" led by militant trades unionists. Formerly a [Liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Party_\(UK\)) stronghold, the industrial districts switched to [Labour](19279158.md) by 1922, with a base among the [Irish Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Catholic) working-class districts. Women were especially active in building neighbourhood solidarity on housing issues. The "Reds" operated within the Labour Party with little influence in Parliament and the mood changed to passive despair by the late 1920s.[^cite_note-96]

During the Second World War, Scotland was targeted by [Nazi Germany](21212.md) largely due to its factories, shipyards, and coal mines.[^cite_note-blitz-97] Cities such as [Glasgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasgow) and [Edinburgh](9602.md) were targeted by German bombers, as were smaller towns mostly located in the central belt of the country.[^cite_note-blitz-97] Perhaps the most significant air raid in Scotland was the [Clydebank Blitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clydebank_Blitz) of March 1941, which intended to destroy naval shipbuilding in the area.[^cite_note-Clydebank_blitz-98] 528 people were killed and 4,000 homes destroyed.[^cite_note-Clydebank_blitz-98] Perhaps Scotland's most unusual wartime episode occurred in 1941 when [Rudolf Hess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolf_Hess) flew to Renfrewshire, possibly intending to broker a peace deal through the [Duke of Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Douglas-Hamilton,_14th_Duke_of_Hamilton).[^cite_note-99] Before his departure from Germany, Hess had given his adjutant, [Karlheinz Pintsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karlheinz_Pintsch), a letter addressed to [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md) that detailed his intentions to open peace negotiations with the British. Pintsch delivered the letter to Hitler at the Berghof around noon on 11 May.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008168-100] [Albert Speer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Speer) later said Hitler described Hess's departure as one of the worst personal blows of his life, as he considered it a personal betrayal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESereny1996240-101] Hitler worried that his allies, Italy and Japan, would perceive Hess's act as an attempt by Hitler to secretly open peace negotiations with the British.



Reconvening of the [Scottish Parliament](61188.md) in July 1999 with [Donald Dewar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Dewar), [first minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_minister_of_Scotland) (left) and [Queen Elizabeth II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_II) (centre)

After 1945, Scotland's economic situation worsened due to overseas competition, inefficient industry, and industrial disputes.[^cite_note-102] Only in recent decades has the country enjoyed something of a cultural and economic renaissance. Economic factors contributing to this recovery included a resurgent financial services industry, [electronics manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronics_manufacturing), (see [Silicon Glen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_Glen)),[^cite_note-103] and the [North Sea oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Sea_oil) and gas industry.[^cite_note-104] The introduction in 1989 by Margaret Thatcher's government of the [Community Charge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poll_tax_\(Great_Britain\)) (widely known as the Poll Tax) one year before the rest of Great Britain,[^cite_note-105] contributed to a growing movement for Scottish control over domestic affairs.[^cite_note-106] On 21 December 1988, [Pan Am Flight 103](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_Am_Flight_103) exploded mid–air over the town of [Lockerbie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockerbie), killing all on board as well as eleven Lockerbie residents. It remains the deadliest [terrorist attack in the United Kingdom](18563581.md).[^cite_note-107]

Following a [referendum on devolution proposals in 1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_devolution_referendum,_1997), the [Scotland Act 1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_Act_1998)[^cite_note-108] was passed by the British Parliament, which established a devolved [Scottish Parliament](61188.md) and [Scottish Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Government) with responsibility for most laws specific to Scotland.[^cite_note-109] The Scottish Parliament was reconvened in [Edinburgh](9602.md) on 4 July 1999.[^cite_note-110] The first to hold the office of [first minister of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_minister_of_Scotland) was [Donald Dewar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Dewar), who served until his sudden death in 2000.[^cite_note-111]

### 21st century



The signing of the [Edinburgh Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edinburgh_Agreement_\(2012\)) permitted a [referendum on independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Scottish_independence_referendum) to be held in 2014

The [Scottish Parliament Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Parliament_Building) at Holyrood opened in October 2004 after lengthy construction delays and running over budget.[^cite_note-112] The Scottish Parliament's form of [proportional representation](23298.md) (the [additional member system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Additional_member_system)) resulted in no one party having an overall majority for the first three Scottish parliament elections.

The pro-[independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_independence) [Scottish National Party](28887.md) led by [Alex Salmond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Salmond) achieved an overall majority in the [2011 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Parliament_election), winning 69 of the 129 seats available.[^cite_note-113] The success of the SNP in achieving a majority in the Scottish Parliament paved the way for the [September 2014 referendum on Scottish independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_independence_referendum,_2014). The majority voted against the proposition, with 55% voting no to independence.[^cite_note-114] More powers, particularly concerning taxation, were devolved to the Scottish Parliament after the referendum, following cross-party talks in the [Smith Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smith_Commission).

Since the 2014 referendum, events such as the UK leaving the European Union, despite a majority of voters in Scotland voting to remain a member, have led to calls for a [second independence referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposed_second_Scottish_independence_referendum). In 2022 the [Lord Advocate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Advocate) [Dorothy Bain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorothy_Bain) argued the case for the Scottish Government to hold another referendum on the issue, with the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_Kingdom) later ruling against the argument.[^cite_note-115] Following the Supreme Court decision, the Scottish Government stated that it wished to make amendments to the [Scotland Act 1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_Act_1998) that would allow a referendum to be held.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-r556-117] On 14th September 2026, First Minister [John Swinney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Swinney) signed a [Memorandum of understanding](1018279.md) alongside the First Ministers of both Wales and Northern Ireland, which called on a future referendum on independence which Swinney claimed was a "pivotal moment in the constitutional journey".[^cite_note-118]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Scotland)



At 4,413 feet (1,345 m), [Ben Nevis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Nevis) is the highest peak in Scotland and the [British Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Isles)

The mainland of Scotland comprises the northern third of the land mass of the island of Great Britain, which lies off the northwest coast of [Continental Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Europe). The total area is 30,977 square miles (80,231 km<sup>2</sup>) with a land area of 30,078 square miles (77,901 km<sup>2</sup>),[^cite_note-ONS_Standard_Area_Measurement-6] comparable to the size of the [Czech Republic](5321.md). Scotland's only land border is with England, and runs for 96 miles (154 km) between the basin of the [River Tweed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Tweed) on the east coast and the [Solway Firth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solway_Firth) in the west. The Atlantic Ocean borders the west coast and the [North Sea](21179.md) is to the east. The island of Ireland lies only 13 miles (21 km) from the south-western peninsula of [Kintyre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kintyre);[^cite_note-119] Norway is 190 miles (305 km) to the northeast and the [Faroe Islands](17238607.md), 168 miles (270 km) to the north.

The territorial extent of Scotland is generally that established by the 1237 [Treaty of York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_York) between Scotland and the Kingdom of England[^cite_note-Scotland_Conquered-53] and the 1266 [Treaty of Perth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Perth) between Scotland and Norway.[^cite_note-Mackie-22] Important exceptions include the [Isle of Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Man), which having been lost to England in the 14th century is now a [crown dependency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_dependency) outside of the United Kingdom; the island groups [Orkney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orkney) and [Shetland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shetland), which were acquired from Norway in 1472;[^cite_note-Whitaker-120] and [Berwick-upon-Tweed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berwick-upon-Tweed) (after changing ownership several times) was ceded to England in 1482.[^cite_note-q088-121]

The geographical [centre of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_of_Scotland) lies a few miles from the village of [Newtonmore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newtonmore) in [Badenoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badenoch).[^cite_note-122] Rising to 4,413 feet (1,345 m) above sea level, Scotland's highest point is the summit of [Ben Nevis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Nevis), in [Lochaber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lochaber), while Scotland's longest river, the [River Tay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Tay), flows for a distance of 117 miles (188 km).[^cite_note-ONS_Geography_Guide-17]

### Islands

Main article: [Islands of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islands_of_Scotland)



The [Quiraing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quiraing) on the [Isle of Skye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Skye), one of the islands of the [Inner Hebrides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_Hebrides)

Scotland has an estimated 900 islands, of which roughly 118 are inhabited.[^cite_note-123] The islands are divided into four main groups: [Shetland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shetland), [Orkney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orkney), the [Inner Hebrides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_Hebrides) and the [Outer Hebrides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_Hebrides). The country has the majority of the largest islands in the British Isles, with 71 with an area of at least 5 km<sup>2</sup> (1.9 sq mi).[^cite_note-Britain's_largest_islands_{{!}}_Blog_{{!}}_OS-124] The largest island is [Lewis and Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_and_Harris), followed by [Skye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skye), the [Shetland mainland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland,_Shetland) and [Mull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Mull).[^cite_note-Britain's_largest_islands_{{!}}_Blog_{{!}}_OS-124] Smaller islands by population include [Arran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Arran), [Bute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Bute), [South Uist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Uist), [Islay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islay) and [Great Cumbrae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Cumbrae) which have populations between 1,300 and 6,400.[^cite_note-5_Exploring_individual_factors:_population,_local_amenities,_and_ferry_connections-125] [Ailsa Craig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ailsa_Craig), which lies off the coast of South Ayrshire, has no permanent population. It is known as a source for [microgranite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Granite), which is used to make [curling stones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curling_stones).[^cite_note-126]

Off the northern coast of the mainland lie [Shetland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shetland) and [Orkney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orkney), two island groups which, despite being part of Scotland since 1472, have retained elements of their own culture and identify. They were acquired by [James III of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_III_of_Scotland) following [King Christian I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Christian_I) of [Denmark](76972.md)'s decision to relinquish the islands.[^cite_note-127] Shetland and Orkney are governed by their own administrative councils and both act as one of the 32 administrative areas of Scotland.[^cite_note-128] [Rockall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockall) is a small rocky islet in the North Atlantic which was declared part of Scotland by the [Island of Rockall Act 1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Island_of_Rockall_Act_1972).[^cite_note-129][^cite_note-130] Despite no possession by any other state or other precedents, the legality of the claim is disputed by the [Republic of Ireland](14560.md), Denmark and [Iceland](14531.md), and may be unenforceable in international law.[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132]

### Geology and geomorphology

Main article: [Geology of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_Scotland)



[Arthur's Seat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur's_Seat), Edinburgh



[Loch Lomond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loch_Lomond), a freshwater [loch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loch), often considered the boundary between the Lowlands and Highlands

[^cite_note-weir-133]

The whole of Scotland was covered by ice sheets during the [Pleistocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pleistocene) [ice ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_ages) and the landscape is much affected by glaciation. From a geological perspective, the country has three main sub-divisions: the Highlands and Islands, the Central Lowlands, and the Southern Uplands.

The [Highlands and Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highlands_and_Islands) lie to the north and west of the [Highland Boundary Fault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_Boundary_Fault), which runs from [Arran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Arran) to [Stonehaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonehaven). This part of Scotland largely comprises ancient rocks from the [Cambrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambrian) and [Precambrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precambrian), which were uplifted during the later [Caledonian orogeny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caledonian_orogeny). It is interspersed with [igneous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igneous) intrusions of a more recent age, remnants of which formed mountain massifs such as the [Cairngorms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairngorms) and the [Cuillin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuillin).[^cite_note-134] In north-eastern mainland Scotland [weathering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weathering) of rock that occurred before the [Last Ice Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Glacial_Period) has shaped much of the landscape.[^cite_note-Hall1986-135]

A significant exception to the above are the fossil-bearing beds of [Old Red Sandstones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Red_Sandstone) found principally along the [Moray Firth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moray_Firth) coast. The [Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Highlands) are generally mountainous and the highest elevations in the British Isles are found here. There are numerous bodies of freshwater including [Loch Lomond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loch_Lomond) and [Loch Ness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loch_Ness). Some parts of the coastline consist of [machair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machair_\(geography\)), a low-lying dune pasture land.

The [Central Lowlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Lowlands) is a [rift valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rift_valley) mainly comprising [Paleozoic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleozoic) formations. Many of these sediments have economic significance, for it is here that the coal and iron-bearing rocks that fuelled Scotland's [industrial revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_revolution) are found. This area has also experienced intense volcanism, [Arthur's Seat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur's_Seat) in Edinburgh being the remnant of a once much larger volcano. This area is relatively low-lying, although even here hills such as the [Ochils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ochil_Hills) and [Campsie Fells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campsie_Fells) are rarely far from view.

The [Southern Uplands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Uplands) is a range of hills almost 125 miles (200 km) long, interspersed with broad valleys. They lie south of a second [fault line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fault_\(geology\)) (the Southern Uplands fault) that runs from [Girvan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Girvan) to [Dunbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunbar).[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137][^cite_note-Scotland_Today_»_ITKT-138] The geological foundations largely comprise [Silurian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silurian) deposits laid down some 400 to 500 million years ago. The high point of the Southern Uplands is [Merrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merrick,_Galloway) with an elevation of 843 m (2,766 ft).[^cite_note-Keay-21][^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141] The Southern Uplands is home to Scotland's highest village, [Wanlockhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wanlockhead) (430 m or 1,411 ft above sea level).[^cite_note-Scotland_Today_»_ITKT-138]

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Scotland)



[Tiree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiree) in the [Inner Hebrides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_Hebrides) is one of the sunniest locations in Scotland.

The climate of most of Scotland is [temperate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate) and [oceanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate), and tends to be very changeable. As it is warmed by the [Gulf Stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Stream) from the Atlantic, it has much milder winters (but cooler, wetter summers) than areas on similar latitudes, such as [Labrador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labrador), southern [Scandinavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavia), the [Moscow region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_region) in [Russia](25391.md), and the [Kamchatka Peninsula](678671.md) on the opposite side of [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia). Temperatures are generally lower than in the rest of the UK, with the temperature of −27.2 °C (−17.0 °F) recorded at [Braemar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braemar) in the [Grampian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grampian_Mountains), on 11 February 1895, the coldest ever recorded anywhere in the UK.[^cite_note-142] The same temperature was also recorded in Braemar on 10 January 1982 and at [Altnaharra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altnaharra), [Highland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_\(council_area\)), on 30 December 1995. Winter maxima average 6 °C (43 °F) in the lowlands, with summer maxima averaging 18 °C (64 °F). The highest temperature recorded was 34.8 °C (94.6 °F) at [Charterhall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAF_Charterhall), [Scottish Borders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Borders) on 19 July 2022.[^cite_note-143]

The west of Scotland is usually warmer than the east, owing to the influence of Atlantic [ocean currents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ocean_currents) and the colder surface temperatures of the [North Sea](21179.md). [Tiree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiree), in the Inner Hebrides, is one of the sunniest places in the country: it had more than 300 hours of sunshine in May 1975.[^cite_note-Metext-144] Rainfall varies widely across Scotland. The western Highlands of Scotland are the wettest, with annual rainfall in a few places exceeding 3,000 mm (120 in).[^cite_note-145] In comparison, much of lowland Scotland receives less than 800 mm (31 in) annually.[^cite_note-Meteast-146] Heavy snowfall is not common in the Lowlands, but becomes more common with altitude. Braemar has an average of 59 snow days per year,[^cite_note-147] while many coastal areas average fewer than 10 days of lying snow per year.[^cite_note-Meteast-146]

[Climate change](5042951.md) is expected to have a significant impact on Scotland; if the world warms by 3 degrees, it could cost up to 8% of the GDP of the United Kingdom by 2070. Between 1990 and 2023, Scotland reduced its [greenhouse gas](21350772.md) emissions by 51.3%. In 2026 it adopted a climate change plan with the goal of becoming carbon neutral by 2045. The plan includes making heating houses carbon-free by 2045, covering 21% of the country by forests by 2032, and restoring [peatlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peatland).[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150]

### Flora and fauna

Main articles: [Fauna of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_Scotland) and [Flora of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora_of_Scotland)



[White-tailed sea eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-tailed_sea_eagle)

Scotland's wildlife is typical of the north-west of Europe, although several of the larger mammals such as the lynx, brown bear, wolf, elk and walrus were hunted to extinction in historic times. There are important populations of seals and internationally significant nesting grounds for a variety of seabirds such as [gannets](251885.md).[^cite_note-151] The [golden eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_eagle) is something of a national icon.[^cite_note-152]

On the high mountain tops, species including [ptarmigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_ptarmigan), [mountain hare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_hare) and [stoat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoat) can be seen in their white colour phase during winter months.[^cite_note-153] Remnants of the native [Scots pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_pine) forest exist[^cite_note-154] and within these areas the [Scottish crossbill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_crossbill), the UK's only [endemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemism) bird species and [vertebrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertebrate), can be found alongside [capercaillie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_capercaillie), [Scottish wildcat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_wildcat), [red squirrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_squirrel) and [pine marten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pine_marten).[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157] Various animals have been re-introduced, including the [white-tailed eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-tailed_eagle) in 1975, the [red kite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_kite) in the 1980s,[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159] and there have been experimental projects involving the [beaver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_beaver) and [wild boar](52255.md), which are both now relatively widespread. Today, much of the remaining native [Caledonian Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caledonian_Forest) lies within the [Cairngorms National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairngorms_National_Park) and remnants of the forest remain at 84 locations across Scotland. On the west coast, remnants of ancient Celtic Rainforest remain, particularly on the Taynish peninsula in [Argyll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argyll), these forests are particularly rare due to high rates of deforestation throughout Scottish history.[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-BBC_News-161] Scotland now ranks in the lowest 15% of countries in the Biodiversity Intactness Index.[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163]

The flora of the country is varied incorporating both [deciduous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deciduous) and [coniferous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coniferous) woodland as well as [moorland](553383.md) and [tundra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tundra) species. Large-scale commercial tree planting and management of upland moorland habitat for the grazing of sheep and [field sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_sports) activities like [deer stalking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deer_stalking) and [driven grouse shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Driven_grouse_shooting) impacts the distribution of [indigenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_\(ecology\)) plants and animals.[^cite_note-164] The UK's tallest tree is a [grand fir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abies_grandis) planted beside [Loch Fyne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loch_Fyne), Argyll in the 1870s, and the [Fortingall Yew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortingall_Yew) may be 5,000 years old and is probably the oldest living thing in Europe.[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167] Although the number of native [vascular plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vascular_plant) is low by world standards, Scotland's substantial [bryophyte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bryophyte) flora is of global importance.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-RBGE-169]

## Government and politics

Main articles: [Politics of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Scotland), [Scottish Parliament](61188.md), and [Scottish Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Government)

Further information: [Political parties in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_parties_in_Scotland), [Elections in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Scotland), and [Electoral system of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_system_of_Scotland)



[King Charles III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Charles_III)\
[Monarch](47063.md)\
since 2022



[John Swinney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Swinney)\
[First Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Minister_of_Scotland)\
since 2024

Scotland is part of the United Kingdom, a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy) whose current sovereign is [Charles III](125248.md).[^cite_note-170] The monarchy uses a variety of styles, titles and other symbols specific to Scotland, most of which originated in the pre–union [Kingdom of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland). These include the [Royal Standard of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Standard_of_Scotland), the [royal coat of arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_coat_of_arms_of_the_United_Kingdom#In_Scotland), and the title [Duke of Rothesay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_of_Rothesay), which is traditionally given to the heir apparent. There are also distinct Scottish [Officers of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Officers_of_State_\(United_Kingdom\)#Scotland) and Officers of the Crown, and the [Order of the Thistle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_the_Thistle), a [chivalric order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_chivalry), is specific to the country.[^cite_note-171]

The [Parliament of the United Kingdom](13964.md) and the [Scottish Parliament](61188.md) are the country's primary legislative bodies. The UK Parliament is [sovereign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_sovereignty) and therefore has supremacy over the Scottish Parliament,[^cite_note-172] but generally restricts itself to legislating over reserved matters: primarily some taxes, some aspects of social security, defence, international relations, and broadcasting.[^cite_note-Gate22-173] There is a [convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_consent_motion) that the UK Parliament will not legislate over devolved matters without the Scottish Parliament's consent.[^cite_note-174] Scotland is represented in the [House of Commons](13828.md), the [lower chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_chamber) of the UK Parliament, by 57 [Members of Parliament](3776360.md) (out of a total of 650).[^cite_note-175] They are elected to single-member [constituencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Westminster_constituencies) under the [first-past-the-post](261709.md) system of voting. The [Scotland Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_Office) represents the British government in Scotland and represents Scottish interests within the government.[^cite_note-176] The Scotland Office is led by the [secretary of state for Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Scotland), who sits in the [Cabinet of the United Kingdom](162790.md).[^cite_note-jack2-177] The Labour MP [Douglas Alexander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Alexander) has held the position since September 2025,[^cite_note-178] replacing [Ian Murray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Murray_\(Scottish_politician\)), who had served from July 2024.[^cite_note-179]



Debating chamber of the [Scottish Parliament](61188.md) in Edinburgh

The Scottish Parliament is a [unicameral](204304.md) legislature with 129 members (MSPs): 73 of them represent individual constituencies and are elected on a [first-past-the-post](261709.md) system, and the other 56 are elected in eight different electoral regions by the [additional member system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Additional_member_system_\(Scottish_Parliament\)). MSPs normally serve for a five-year period.[^cite_note-180] The Scottish Parliament has the most extensive [devolved powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolved,_reserved_and_excepted_matters) amongst the [devolved parliaments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution_in_the_United_Kingdom) in the United Kingdom,[^cite_note-181] and considered one of the most powerful devolved parliaments internationally.[^cite_note-182]

The [Scottish Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Government), which has been described as one of the most powerful devolved governments globally,[^cite_note-183] is led by the [first minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Minister_of_Scotland), who is nominated by MSPs and is typically the leader of the largest party in the Parliament. Other ministers are appointed by the first minister and serve at their discretion.[^cite_note-184] As the head of the Scottish Government, the first minister is responsible for the comprehensive development, implementation and presentation of government policy, and is responsible for promoting the interests of the country at home and internationally.[^cite_note-185] [John Swinney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Swinney), leader of the Scottish National Party (SNP), has served as the first minister since 8 May 2024.[^cite_note-186]

### Diplomacy and relations

Main articles: [International relations of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_relations_of_Scotland) and [List of consular missions in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_consular_missions_in_Scotland)

Further information: [Intergovernmental relations in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intergovernmental_relations_in_the_United_Kingdom)



First Minister Swinney meets with U.S. [President](24113.md) [Donald Trump](4848272.md), September 2025



First Minister Sturgeon holds a bilateral meeting with Icelandic [Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Iceland) [Katrín Jakobsdóttir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katr%C3%ADn_Jakobsd%C3%B3ttir), April 2019



First Minister Swinney hosts [President of Ireland](24676.md) [Catherine Connolly](25296931.md) in Edinburgh, September 2026

As leader of the Scottish Government, the first minister is a member of the [Prime Minister and Heads of Devolved Governments Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_and_Heads_of_Devolved_Governments_Council) and the [Council of the Nations and Regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_the_Nations_and_Regions), the bodies which facilitate intergovernmental relations within the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-187] Foreign policy is a reserved matter and primarily the responsibility of the [Foreign Office](59653.md), a department of the UK Government.[^cite_note-188] However, the Scottish Government may promote Scottish interests abroad and encourage foreign investment in Scotland.[^cite_note-189] The first minister and the [constitution secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_secretary) have portfolios which include foreign affairs.[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191][^cite_note-assets.publishing.service.gov.uk-192]

Scotland's [international network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_relations_of_Scotland) consists of two Scotland Houses, one in [Brussels](3708.md) and the other in [London](17867.md), seven Scottish Government international offices, and over thirty Scottish Development International offices in other countries globally.
