Both Scotland Houses are independent Scottish Government establishments, whilst the seven Scottish Government international offices are based in [British embassies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_diplomatic_missions_of_the_United_Kingdom) or British High Commission offices.[^cite_note-report-2022-23-193] The Scottish Government has a network of offices in Beijing, Berlin, Brussels, Copenhagen, Dublin, London, Ottawa, Paris, and Washington, D.C., which promote Scottish interests in their respective areas.[^cite_note-194] Additionally, the country has a number of [foreign diplomatic missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_consular_missions_in_Scotland), most of which are located in the capital city, Edinburgh.[^cite_note-195]

The nation has historic ties to France as a result of the '[Auld Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auld_Alliance)', a treaty signed between the Kingdom of Scotland and [Kingdom of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_France) in 1295 to discourage an English invasion of either country.[^cite_note-bbc.co.uk-196] The alliance effectively ended in the sixteenth century, but the two countries continue to have a close relationship, with a Statement of Intent being signed in 2013 between the Scottish Government and the [Government of France](3398091.md).[^cite_note-197] In 2004 the [Scotland Malawi Partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_Malawi_Partnership) was established, which co-ordinates Scottish activities to strengthen existing links with [Malawi](19088.md), and in 2021, the Scottish Government and [Government of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Ireland) signed the Ireland-Scotland Bilateral Review, committing both governments to increased levels of co-operation on areas such as diplomacy, economy and business.[^cite_note-report-2022-23-193][^cite_note-198][^cite_note-firstminister.gov.scot-199] Scotland also has [historical and cultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavian_Scotland) ties with the [Scandinavian countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavian_countries).[^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201] Scottish Government policy advocates for stronger political relations with the [Nordic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_countries) and [Baltic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_states) countries, which has resulted in some Nordic-inspired policies being adopted such as baby boxes.[^cite_note-\:11-202][^cite_note-203] Representatives from the Scottish Parliament attended the [Nordic Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Council) as an observer for the first time in 2022.[^cite_note-204]

Scotland is a member of the [British–Irish Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British%E2%80%93Irish_Council), the [Conference of European Regions with Legislative Power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/REGLEG) (REGLEG), the [British–Irish Parliamentary Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British%E2%80%93Irish_Parliamentary_Assembly), the [Council of European Municipalities and Regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_European_Municipalities_and_Regions),[^cite_note-205] the [Inter-Parliamentary Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-Parliamentary_Forum), the [Congress of Local and Regional Authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Local_and_Regional_Authorities),[^cite_note-206] the [Commonwealth Parliamentary Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Parliamentary_Association)[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209] and the [Parliamentary Partnership Assembly with the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU%E2%80%93UK_Parliamentary_Partnership_Assembly).[^cite_note-210] Scotland held the Presidency of Conference of European Regions with Legislative Power from November 2003 until November 2004 during the [premiership of Jack McConnell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premiership_of_Jack_McConnell).[^cite_note-211]

### Devolution and independence

Main articles: [Scottish devolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_devolution), [Scottish nationalism](818138.md), and [Scottish independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_independence)



[Donald Dewar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Dewar), the inaugural [first minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Minister_of_Scotland) and was referred to as the "[Father of the Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Father_of_the_Nation)"

[^cite_note-212]

[Devolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution)—the granting of central government powers to a regional government[^cite_note-213]– gained increasing popularity as a policy in the United Kingdom in the late twentieth century; it was described by [John Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Smith_\(Labour_Party_leader\)), then [Leader of the Labour Party](19043368.md), as the "settled will of the Scottish people".[^cite_note-214] The Scottish Parliament and [Scottish Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Government) were subsequently established under the [Scotland Act 1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_Act_1998); the Act followed a successful [referendum in 1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_devolution_referendum,_1997) which found majority support for both creating the Parliament and granting it limited powers to [vary income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_variable_rate).[^cite_note-215] The Act enabled the new institutions to legislate in all areas not explicitly reserved by the UK Parliament.[^cite_note-Devolved_and_Reserved_Powers-216]

Two more pieces of legislation, the Scotland Acts of [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_Act_2012) and [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_Act_2016), gave the Scottish Parliament further powers to legislate on taxation and social security;[^cite_note-217] the 2016 Act also gave the Scottish Government powers to manage the affairs of the [Crown Estate in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Estate_Scotland).[^cite_note-218] Conversely, the [United Kingdom Internal Market Act 2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_Internal_Market_Act_2020) constrains the Scottish Parliament's ability to regulate goods and services,[^cite_note-MaMu22-219][^cite_note-DouHu22-220] and thus it undermines devolution.[^cite_note-226]

The [2007 Scottish Parliament elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Parliament_general_election,_2007) led to the [Scottish National Party](28887.md) (SNP), which supports [Scottish independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_independence), forming a [minority government](271434.md). The new government established a "[National Conversation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Conversation)" on constitutional issues, proposing a number of options such as increasing the powers of the Scottish Parliament, [federalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation), or a referendum on Scottish independence from the United Kingdom. The three main unionist opposition parties–[Scottish Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Labour), the [Scottish Conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Conservatives), and the [Scottish Liberal Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Liberal_Democrats)–created a separate [commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calman_Commission) to investigate the distribution of powers between devolved Scottish and UK-wide bodies while not considering independence.[^cite_note-227] In August 2009 the SNP proposed a bill to hold a referendum on independence in November 2010, but was defeated by opposition from all other major parties.[^cite_note-ReferendumBill2010-228][^cite_note-Times3Sep09-229][^cite_note-230]



First Minister [Nicola Sturgeon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicola_Sturgeon) with [Michel Barnier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Barnier) in [Brussels](3708.md), to discuss Scottish affairs following [Brexit](41688778.md), 2019

The [2011 Scottish Parliament election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Scottish_Parliament_election) resulted in an SNP overall majority in the Scottish Parliament, and on 18 September 2014 a [referendum on Scottish independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Scottish_independence_referendum) was held.[^cite_note-231] The referendum resulted in a rejection of independence, by 55.3% to 44.7%.[^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233] During the campaign, the three main parties in the British Parliament–the [Conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)), [Labour](19279158.md), and the [Liberal Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_\(UK\))–pledged to extend the powers of the Scottish Parliament.[^cite_note-234][^cite_note-kelvin-235] An all-party [commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smith_Commission) chaired by [Robert Smith, Baron Smith of Kelvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Smith,_Baron_Smith_of_Kelvin) was formed,[^cite_note-kelvin-235] which led to the Scotland Act 2016.[^cite_note-236]

Following the [European Union Referendum Act 2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union_Referendum_Act_2015), the [2016 United Kingdom European Union membership referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_Kingdom_European_Union_membership_referendum) was held on 23 June 2016 on Britain's membership of the [European Union](9317.md). A majority in the United Kingdom voted to withdraw from the EU, while a majority within Scotland voted to remain a member.[^cite_note-\:2-237] The first minister, Nicola Sturgeon, announced the following day that as a result a [new independence referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposed_second_Scottish_independence_referendum) was "highly likely".[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-\:2-237] On 31 January 2020 the United Kingdom formally withdrew from the European Union.

### Local government

Main articles: [Local government in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_Scotland) and [Subdivisions of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subdivisions_of_Scotland)



Cunninghame House in [Irvine, North Ayrshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irvine,_North_Ayrshire), seat of [North Ayrshire Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Ayrshire_Council)

For [local government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_Scotland) purposes Scotland is subdivided into 32 single-tier council areas.[^cite_note-239] The areas were established in 1996, and their councils are responsible for the provision of all local government services. Decisions are made by councillors, who are elected at [local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Scotland) every five years. The leader of the council is typically a councillor from the party with the most seats; councils also have a civic head, typically called the [provost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provost_\(civil\)) or [lord provost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_provost), who represents the council on ceremonial occasions and chairs council meetings.[^cite_note-240] [Community Councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_council) are informal organisations that represent smaller subdivisions within each council area.[^cite_note-\:9-241]

[Police Scotland](34423036.md) and the [Scottish Fire and Rescue Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Fire_and_Rescue_Service) cover the entire country. For healthcare and postal districts, and a number of other governmental and non-governmental organisations such as the churches, there are other long-standing methods of subdividing Scotland for the purposes of administration.

There are [eight cities in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cities_of_Scotland): [Aberdeen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aberdeen), [Dundee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dundee), [Dunfermline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunfermline), [Edinburgh](9602.md), [Glasgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasgow), [Inverness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inverness), [Perth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth,_Scotland) and [Stirling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stirling).[^cite_note-242] [City status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_status_in_the_United_Kingdom) is conferred by the monarch through [letters patent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letters_patent).[^cite_note-243]

### Military

Main article: [Armed forces in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_forces_in_Scotland)

Further information: [Scots Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_Army), [Royal Scots Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Scots_Navy), and [Military history of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Scotland)



A [Typhoon FGR4](167667.md) in [No. 6 Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._6_Squadron_RAF) markings at [RAF Lossiemouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAF_Lossiemouth)

As one of the countries of the United Kingdom, the [British Armed Forces](31730.md) are the armed forces of Scotland. Of the money spent on UK defence, about £3.3 billion could be attributed to Scotland in 2018–2019.[^cite_note-244] Scotland had a long military tradition predating the [Treaty of Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Union) with England. Following the Treaty of Union in 1707, the [Scots Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_Army) and [Royal Scots Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Scots_Navy) merged with their English counterparts to form the [Royal Navy](26061.md) and the [British Army](4887.md), which together form part of the British Armed Forces.[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246] The [Atholl Highlanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atholl_Highlanders), Europe's only remaining legal [private army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_army), did not join the Scots Army or Royal Scots Navy in merging with English armed forces, remaining a private army not under the command of the British Armed Forces.[^cite_note-247]

Numerous [Scottish regiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_regiment) have at various times existed in the British Army. Distinctively Scottish regiments in the British Army include the [Scots Guards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_Guards), the [Royal Scots Dragoon Guards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Scots_Dragoon_Guards) and the [154 (Scottish) Regiment RLC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/154_\(Scottish\)_Regiment_RLC), an [Army Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_Reserve_\(United_Kingdom\)) regiment of the [Royal Logistic Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Logistic_Corps). In 2006, as a result of the *[Delivering Security in a Changing World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delivering_Security_in_a_Changing_World)* [white paper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_paper), the Scottish infantry regiments in the [Scottish Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Division) were amalgamated to form the [Royal Regiment of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Regiment_of_Scotland).[^cite_note-248] As a result of the [Cameron–Clegg coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameron%E2%80%93Clegg_coalition)'s [Strategic Defence and Security Review 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Defence_and_Security_Review_2010), the Scottish regiments of the line in the [British Army](4887.md) [infantry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infantry_of_the_British_Army), having previously formed the Scottish Division, were reorganised into the [Scottish, Welsh and Irish Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish,_Welsh_and_Irish_Division) in 2017. Before the formation of the Scottish Division, the Scottish infantry was organised into a [Lowland Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lowland_Brigade_\(United_Kingdom\)) and [Highland Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_Brigade_\(United_Kingdom\)).[^cite_note-249]

&#x20;Because of their topography and perceived remoteness, parts of Scotland have housed many sensitive defence establishments.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252] Between 1960 and 1991 the [Holy Loch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Loch) was a base for the US fleet of [Polaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polaris_ballistic_missile)-equipped [ballistic missile submarines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballistic_missile_submarine).[^cite_note-253] Today, [His Majesty's Naval Base Clyde](1706832.md), 25 miles (40 kilometres) north-west of Glasgow, is the base for the four [Trident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trident_\(missile\))-armed [*Vanguard*-class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanguard-class_submarine) ballistic missile submarines that comprise the Britain's [nuclear deterrent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_deterrent).



A [Challenger 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Challenger_2) [main battle tank](26595097.md) of the [Royal Scots Dragoon Guards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Scots_Dragoon_Guards)

Scotland's [Scapa Flow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scapa_Flow) was the main base for the [Royal Navy](26061.md) in the 20th century.[^cite_note-254] As the [Cold War](325329.md) intensified in 1961, the United States deployed [Polaris ballistic missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polaris_ballistic_missile), and submarines, in the [Firth of Clyde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firth_of_Clyde)'s [Holy Loch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Loch). Public protests from [CND](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CND) campaigners proved futile. The Royal Navy successfully convinced the government to allow the base because it wanted its own Polaris submarines, and it obtained them in 1963. The RN's nuclear submarine base opened with four [*Resolution*-class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resolution-class_submarine) Polaris submarines at the expanded [Faslane Naval Base](1706832.md) on the [Gare Loch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gare_Loch). The first patrol of a [Trident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trident_missile)-armed submarine occurred in 1994, although the US base was closed at the end of the Cold War.[^cite_note-255]

A single front-line [Royal Air Force](25679.md) base is located in Scotland. [RAF Lossiemouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAF_Lossiemouth), located in [Moray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moray), is the most northerly [air defence](146640.md) [fighter](10929.md) base in the United Kingdom and is home to four [Eurofighter Typhoon](167667.md) combat aircraft squadrons, three [Poseidon MRA1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poseidon_MRA1) squadrons, and a full–time, permanently based RAF Regiment squadron.[^cite_note-256] An additional four RAF Reserve Squadrons are based in Scotland – 612 Squadron, 2622 (Highland) Squadron and 602 Squadron in Glasgow, and 603 Squadron in Edinburgh.[^cite_note-257]

### Law and order

Main article: [Scots law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_law)



[Parliament House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_House,_Edinburgh) in Edinburgh, the former [Parliament of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Scotland), now houses the [Supreme Courts of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_of_Justice)

Scots law has a basis derived from [Roman law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_law),[^cite_note-258] combining features of both uncodified [civil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_law_\(legal_system\)), dating back to the *[Corpus Juris Civilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corpus_Juris_Civilis)*, and [common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law) with [medieval sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_institutions_of_Scotland_in_the_High_Middle_Ages). The terms of the Treaty of Union with England in 1707 guaranteed the continued existence of a separate legal system in Scotland from that of England and Wales.[^cite_note-259] Prior to 1611 there were several regional law systems in Scotland, most notably [Udal law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Udal_law) in [Orkney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orkney) and [Shetland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shetland), based on old Norse law. Various other systems derived from common [Celtic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_law) or [Brehon laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brehon_laws) survived in the Highlands until the 1800s.[^cite_note-260] Scots law provides for three types of [courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courts_of_Scotland) responsible for the administration of justice: civil, criminal and [heraldic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Arms). The supreme civil court is the [Court of Session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Session), although civil [appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appeal_\(law\)) can be taken to the [Supreme Court of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_Kingdom) (or before 1 October 2009, the [House of Lords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_functions_of_the_House_of_Lords)). The [High Court of Justiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Court_of_Justiciary) is the supreme criminal court in Scotland. The Court of Session is housed at [Parliament House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_House,_Edinburgh), in Edinburgh, which was the home of the pre-Union [Parliament of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Scotland) with the High Court of Justiciary and the Supreme Court of Appeal currently located at the [Lawnmarket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawnmarket). The [sheriff court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheriff_court) is the main criminal and civil court, hearing most cases. There are 49 sheriff courts throughout the country.[^cite_note-261] [District courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_Courts_of_Scotland) were introduced in 1975 for minor offences and small claims. These were gradually replaced by [Justice of the Peace Courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_of_the_Peace_Court) from 2008 to 2010.

For three centuries the Scots legal system was unique for being the only national legal system without a parliament. This ended with the advent of the Scottish Parliament in 1999, which legislates for devolved matters.[^cite_note-Devolved_and_Reserved_Powers-216] Many features within the system have been preserved. Within criminal law, the Scots legal system is unique in having three possible verdicts: "guilty", "not guilty" and "*[not proven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Not_proven)*".[^cite_note-262] Both "not guilty" and "not proven" result in an [acquittal](300834.md), typically with no possibility of retrial per the rule of [double jeopardy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_jeopardy). A retrial can hear new evidence at a later date that might have proven conclusive in the earlier trial at first instance, where the person acquitted subsequently admits the offence or where it can be proved that the acquittal was tainted by an attempt to [pervert the course of justice](405736.md). Scots juries, sitting in criminal cases, consist of fifteen jurors, which is three more than is typical in many countries.[^cite_note-263]



The [Justiciary Buildings, Glasgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justiciary_Buildings,_Glasgow), which operates as part of the [High Court of Justiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Court_of_Justiciary) along with similar facilities in Edinburgh and Aberdeen

The [Lord Advocate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Advocate) is the chief legal officer of the [Scottish Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Government) and [the Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crown) in Scotland. The Lord Advocate is the head of the systems in Scotland for the investigation and prosecution of crime, the investigation of deaths as well as serving as the principal legal adviser to the Scottish Government and representing the government in legal proceedings.[^cite_note-auto1-264] They are the chief [public prosecutor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_prosecutor) for Scotland and all [prosecutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosecution) on indictment are conducted by the [Crown Office and Procurator Fiscal Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Office_and_Procurator_Fiscal_Service) in the Lord Advocate's name on behalf of the Monarch.[^cite_note-auto1-264] The officeholder is one of the [Great Officers of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Officers_of_State_\(United_Kingdom\)#Scotland) of Scotland. The current Lord Advocate, [Dorothy Bain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorothy_Bain), was nominated by first minister Nicola Sturgeon, and was appointed in June 2021.[^cite_note-265] The Lord Advocate is supported by the [Solicitor General for Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solicitor_General_for_Scotland).[^cite_note-266]

Since 2013, Scotland has had a unified police force known as [Police Scotland](34423036.md). The [Scottish Prison Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Prison_Service) (SPS) manages the prisons in Scotland, which collectively house over 8,500 prisoners.[^cite_note-267] The [Cabinet Secretary for Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_Secretary_for_Justice) is responsible for the Scottish Prison Service within the Scottish Government.

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Scotland)

Further information: [Economic history of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_Scotland), [List of companies of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_companies_of_Scotland), [Taxation in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_Scotland), and [Scottish budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_budget)



[Edinburgh](9602.md), the 13th-largest financial centre in the world and 4th largest in Europe in 2020

[^cite_note-268]

Scotland has a Western-style [open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_economy) [mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy), considered one of the leading [financial centres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_centre) in Europe, and is the largest financial centre in the United Kingdom outside of London.[^cite_note-269] Edinburgh is the financial services centre of Scotland, with large finance firms based there including [Lloyds Banking Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloyds_Banking_Group), the [Bank of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Scotland), the [Royal Bank of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Bank_of_Scotland) and [Standard Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Life).[^cite_note-270] Edinburgh was ranked 15th in the [Global Financial Centres Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Financial_Centres_Index) in 2007,[^cite_note-271] and in 2024 was ranked 33rd internationally and 10th across Europe.[^cite_note-Scottish_cities_rise_in_Global_Financial_Centres_Index_as_industry_reports_13,000_new_jobs-272] Glasgow, Scotland's largest city, also ranks as a significant financial centre in the country, ranked 42nd internationally in 2024 and is widely regarded as an established economic player with particular strengths in domestic expertise.[^cite_note-Scottish_cities_rise_in_Global_Financial_Centres_Index_as_industry_reports_13,000_new_jobs-272] Scotland is ranked within the top 10 of the largest European regions for foreign direct investment, and is rated as one "of the most promising investment destinations in Europe".[^cite_note-273]



[Scotch whisky](28896.md) production at [Bruichladdich distillery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruichladdich_distillery)

In 2024, Scotland's gross domestic product (GDP), including offshore oil and gas, was estimated at £223.4 billion.[^cite_note-Scot_Gov_GDP-11] In 2021 Scottish exports in goods and services (excluding intra-UK trade) were estimated to be £50.1 billion.[^cite_note-ONS_international_trade-274] Scotland's primary goods exports are mineral fuels, machinery and transport, and beverages and tobacco.[^cite_note-HMRC_goods_trade-275] The country's largest export markets in goods are the [Netherlands](21148.md) (£6.9 billion), the [United States](3434750.md) (£4.0 billion), the [Republic of Ireland](14560.md) (£2.8 billion), [Germany](11867.md) (£2.8 billion), [France](5843419.md) (£2.3 billion) and [China](5405.md) (£0.8 billion).[^cite_note-276] Its largest international export market is the [European Union](9317.md).[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278]

[Scotch whisky](28896.md) is one of Scotland's more known goods of economic activity.[^cite_note-Facts_&_Figures-279] In 2024, whisky exports from Scotland contributed £5.4 billion in revenue towards the economy, accounting for 74% of all Scottish food and drink exports internationally in 2023.[^cite_note-Facts_&_Figures-279] The Scotch whisky industry supports over 41,000 jobs in the sector across Scotland.[^cite_note-Facts_&_Figures-279] A considerable number of [Scottish companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_companies_of_Scotland) have established international reputations and trade worldwide in a variety of sectors, including [Rockstar North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockstar_North) (gaming), [Pringle of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pringle_of_Scotland) and [Lyle & Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyle_&_Scott) (fashion and textiles), [A.G. Barr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A.G._Barr) (soft drinks manufacturing), [Alexander Dennis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Dennis) (bus manufacturing), [Stagecoach Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stagecoach_Group) (transportation), [Tunnock's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunnock's) (confectionery) and [Johnnie Walker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnnie_Walker) (whisky).



[Oil extraction in Scottish waters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_industry_in_Scotland) in the [North Sea](21179.md)

Scotland was one of the industrial powerhouses of Europe from the time of the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) onwards, being a world leader in manufacturing.[^cite_note-280] This left a legacy in the diversity of goods and services which Scotland produces, from textiles, [whisky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whisky) and [shortbread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortbread) to jet engines, buses, computer software, [investment management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Investment_management) and other related financial services.[^cite_note-281] In common with most other advanced industrialised economies, Scotland has seen a decline in the importance of both manufacturing industries and primary-based extractive industries. This has been combined with a rise in the [service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_sector_of_the_economy) sector of the economy, which has grown to be the largest sector in Scotland.[^cite_note-282]

Historically, the Scottish economy was dominated by [heavy industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_industry) underpinned by shipbuilding in Glasgow, coal mining and [steel industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel_making). Petroleum-related industries associated with the extraction of [North Sea oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Sea_oil) have also been important employers since the 1970s, especially in the north-east of Scotland. De-industrialisation during the 1970s and 1980s saw a shift from a manufacturing focus towards a more [service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_sector_of_economic_activity)-oriented economy. As a result, the country's primary economic sectors include [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Scotland), [forestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forestry_in_Scotland), [fisheries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishing_in_Scotland), manufacturing and construction, [oil and gas extraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_industry_in_Scotland), science, technology, financial services, [food and drink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuisine_of_Scotland) and [tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Scotland), with the [space industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_industry_of_Scotland), [renewable energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Scotland) and [financial technologies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_technology) sectors being considered as developing industries across the country.[^cite_note-283] Scotland's fisheries sector and associated zone is the fourth largest amongst European nations.[^cite_note-284]

The [Scottish National Investment Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_National_Investment_Bank) was established in 2020 by the Scottish Government; this bank uses public money to fund commercial projects across Scotland in the hope that this seed capital will encourage further private investment. £2 billion of taxpayers' money was earmarked for the bank.[^cite_note-bbc-285]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Scotland)



[The Kelpies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Kelpies), a major attraction in [Falkirk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falkirk)

Tourism and major international events are regarded as one of the country's most important sectors of industry,[^cite_note-Tourism_and_events-286] supporting over 245,000 related jobs across the sector and contributing £10.8 billion in revenue in 2023, 6% of the overall Scottish economy.[^cite_note-287] The most visited tourist attractions in the country include [Edinburgh Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edinburgh_Castle), the [National Museum of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Scotland), the [Battle of Culloden Visitor Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Culloden), [Edinburgh Zoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edinburgh_Zoo), [Stirling Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stirling_Castle) and [Urquhart Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urquhart_Castle).[^cite_note-288] The Tourist Boards (Scotland) Act 2006 established [VisitScotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VisitScotland), the executive agency of the Scottish Government responsible for tourism across the country.[^cite_note-Tourism_and_events-286] An international poll conducted in 2023 ranked Scotland's landscape 7th out of 60 countries, whilst it scored 72.3% for tourism.[^cite_note-289] Edinburgh Castle and the National Museum of Scotland are amongst the most popular attractions in the country, with both receiving over 2 million visitors in 2017.[^cite_note-290]

Over 4 million people visited Scotland in 2023, an increase of 15% since 2019.[^cite_note-291] Attractions such as [Loch Lomond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loch_Lomond), [The Kelpies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Kelpies) and the mythical [Loch Ness Monster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loch_Ness_Monster) attracted visitors primarily from [North America](21139.md).[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293] The majority of international visitors to Scotland are primarily from the [United States](3434750.md), [Germany](11867.md), [Canada](5042916.md) and [Australia](4689264.md).[^cite_note-294] Its coastline, beaches, castles, ancient heritage and cultural attractions have made Scotland one of the most popular destinations in Europe.[^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296] In 2016, expenditure from international tourism accounted for £9.7 billion of the Scottish economy.[^cite_note-297]

### Banking and currency

Main article: [Banknotes of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banknotes_of_Scotland)



The [Bank of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Scotland) is one of the oldest banks in the world, and the first bank in Europe to successfully print its own [banknotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banknotes)

Although the [Bank of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_England) is the [central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_bank) for the UK, three Scottish [clearing banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clearing_bank_\(United_Kingdom\)) issue [Sterling](270673.md) [banknotes](208286.md): the [Bank of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Scotland), the [Royal Bank of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Bank_of_Scotland) and the [Clydesdale Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clydesdale_Bank). The Bank of Scotland was the first bank in Europe to successfully print its own paper currency, with the new banknotes entering circulation in 1696, making the Bank of Scotland the longest continuous issuer of banknotes in the world.[^cite_note-298]

The Royal Bank of Scotland expanded internationally to be the second-largest bank in Europe, the fifth largest in the world by [market capitalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_capitalisation) in 2002,[^cite_note-299] but collapsed in the 2008 financial crisis and had to be bailed out by the UK Government at a cost of £76 billion;[^cite_note-300] its new global headquarters in Edinburgh augmented the city's position as a major financial centre. Prior to the 2008 financial crisis, Scotland ranked second only to London in the European league of headquarters locations of the 30 largest banks in Europe as measured by market value.[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-lloyds-302]

The issuing of banknotes by [retail banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retail_bank) in Scotland is subject to the [Banking Act 2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_Act_2009), which repealed all earlier legislation under which banknote issuance was regulated, and the Scottish and Northern Ireland Banknote Regulations 2009.[^cite_note-303] The value of the Scottish banknotes in circulation in 2013 was £3.8 billion, underwritten by the Bank of England using funds deposited by each clearing bank, under the Banking Act 2009, to cover the total value of such notes in circulation.[^cite_note-Banknotes-304]

### Infrastructure and transportation

Main article: [Transport in Scotland](2938165.md)

Further information: [List of airports in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Scotland) and [List of ports and harbours in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ports_and_harbours_in_Scotland)



[Barra Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_Airport), the only airport in the world to use a [tidal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tide) [beach](40630.md) as the runway



The [Forth Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forth_Bridge), a well-known structure in Scottish rail and a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md)

Scotland has [five international airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_the_United_Kingdom_and_the_British_Crown_Dependencies#Scotland) operating scheduled services to Europe, North America and Asia, as well as domestic services to England, Northern Ireland and Wales and within Scotland.[^cite_note-305] [Highlands and Islands Airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highlands_and_Islands_Airports) operates eleven airports across the [Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Highlands), [Orkney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orkney), [Shetland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shetland) and the [Western Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Isles), which are primarily used for short distance, public service operations, although Inverness Airport has a number of scheduled flights to destinations across the UK and mainland Europe.[^cite_note-306] Edinburgh Airport is currently Scotland's busiest airport, handling over 13 million passengers in 2017.[^cite_note-307] It is also the UK's 6th busiest airport. The airline [Loganair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loganair) has its headquarters at Glasgow Airport and markets itself as *Scotland's Airline*.[^cite_note-308]

[Network Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Network_Rail) owns and operates the fixed infrastructure assets of the railway system in Scotland, while the Scottish Government retains overall responsibility for rail strategy and funding in Scotland.[^cite_note-Office_of_Rail_Regulation-309] Scotland's rail network has 359 railway stations and around 1,710 miles (2,760 km) of track.[^cite_note-310] In 2022–23 there were 64 million passenger journeys on [ScotRail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ScotRail) rail services.[^cite_note-311] On 1 January 2006, [Transport Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_Scotland) was established, which would oversee the regulation of railways in Scotland and administer major rail projects.[^cite_note-TransportSco-312] Since April 2022, Transport Scotland has taken ScotRail back into public ownership via its [operator of last resort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operator_of_last_resort), [Scottish Rail Holdings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Rail_Holdings).[^cite_note-313] It did the same with the Caledonian Sleeper service in June 2023.[^cite_note-ts323-314]

The [Glasgow Subway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasgow_Subway) is the only [underground system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit) in Scotland. It opened on 14 December 1896, making it the third-oldest underground network in the world after the [Budapest Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budapest_Metro) and the [London Underground](17839.md). It is owned and operated by [Strathclyde Partnership for Transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strathclyde_Partnership_for_Transport).[^cite_note-315] The Scottish motorways and major [trunk roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trunk_roads) are managed by Transport Scotland. The remainder of the road network is managed by the [Scottish local authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_local_authorities) in each of their areas.

Regular ferry services operate between the Scottish mainland and outlying islands. Ferries serving both the [Inner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_Hebrides) and [Outer Hebrides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_Hebrides) are principally operated by the [state-owned enterprise](204768.md) [Caledonian MacBrayne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caledonian_MacBrayne).[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317] Services to the [Northern Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Isles) are operated by [Serco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serco). Other routes, such as southwest Scotland to Northern Ireland, are served by multiple companies.[^cite_note-318] [DFDS Seaways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DFDS_Seaways) operated a freight-only [Rosyth – Zeebrugge ferry service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosyth_%E2%80%93_Zeebrugge_ferry_service), until a fire damaged the vessel DFDS were using.[^cite_note-319] A passenger service was also operated between 2002 and 2010.[^cite_note-320]

### Science, technology and energy

Further information: [Nuclear power in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_in_Scotland), [Renewable energy in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_in_Scotland), [Oil industry in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_industry_in_Scotland), [Energy in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Scotland), and [Space industry of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_industry_of_Scotland)

A donkey

Sir [Alexander Fleming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Fleming), the discoverer of the world's first broadly effective [antibiotic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antibiotic) substance – [penicillin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penicillin)

A trout

Scottish inventor [John Logie Baird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Logie_Baird) demonstrated the first working television system on 26 January 1926.

[^cite_note-321]

Scotland's primary sources of energy are provided through renewable energy (61.8%), nuclear (25.7%) and fossil fuel generation (10.9%).[^cite_note-\:10-322] [Whitelee Wind Farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitelee_Wind_Farm) is the largest onshore [wind farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_farm) in the United Kingdom, and was Europe's largest onshore wind farm for some time.[^cite_note-323] [Tidal power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_power) is an emerging source of energy in Scotland. The [MeyGen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MeyGen) tidal stream energy plant in the north of the country is claimed to be the largest tidal stream energy project in the world.[^cite_note-324] In Scotland, 98.6% of all electricity used was from renewable sources. This is minus net exports.[^cite_note-\:10-322] Between October 2021 and September 2022 63.1% of all electricity generated in Scotland was from renewable sources, 83.6% was classed as low carbon and 14.5% was from fossil fuels.[^cite_note-325] The Scottish Government has a target to have the equivalent of 50% of the energy for Scotland's heat, transport and electricity consumption to be supplied from renewable sources by 2030.[^cite_note-326] They have stated that, in 2022, the equivalent of 113% of the country's overall electrical consumption was produced by renewable energy, making it the highest recorded figure of renewable energy generated to date.[^cite_note-327]

[Scotland's inventions and discoveries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Scottish_inventions_and_discoveries) are said to have revolutionised human technology and have played a major role in the creation of the modern world. Such inventions – the [television](29831.md), the [telephone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telephone), [refrigerators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refrigerators), the [MRI scanner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MRI_scanner), [flushing toilets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flushing_toilet) and the [steam engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steam_engine) – are said to have been possible by Scotland's universities and parish schools, together with the commitment Scots had to education during the [Scottish Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Enlightenment).[^cite_note-328] [Alexander Fleming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Fleming) is responsible for the discovery of the world's first broadly effective [antibiotic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antibiotic) substance, which he named [penicillin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penicillin), earning him a [Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine](52502.md) in 1945.[^cite_note-lesprixnobel-329][^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331] Modern Scottish inventions – the [Falkirk Wheel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falkirk_Wheel) and the [Glasgow Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasgow_Tower) – hold world records for being the only rotating [boat lift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boat_lift) and the tallest fully rotating freestanding structure in the world respectively.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333]

Scotland's [space industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_industry_of_Scotland) is a world leader in sustainable space technology,[^cite_note-auto-334][^cite_note-335] and, according to the [UK Space Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_Space_Agency), there are 173 space companies currently operating in Scotland as of May 2021.[^cite_note-336] These include spacecraft manufacturers, launch providers, downstream data analysers, and research organisations.[^cite_note-337] The space industry in Scotland is projected to generate £2billion in income for Scotland's space cluster by 2030.[^cite_note-auto-334] Scottish space industry jobs represent almost one in five of all UK space industry employment.[^cite_note-338] In addition to its space industry, Scotland is home to two planned [spaceports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaceports) – [Sutherland spaceport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sutherland_spaceport) and [SaxaVord Spaceport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SaxaVord_Spaceport).[^cite_note-339] The role of [Astronomer Royal for Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomer_Royal_for_Scotland) was established in 1834, and is now an [honorary title](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honorary_title) granted by the monarch to a distinguished astronomer in recognition of his or her contribution to astronomy in Scotland, and is a figurehead for public engagement and understanding of science and astronomy in the country.[^cite_note-340]

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Scotland)

See also: [Languages of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Scotland), [Religion in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Scotland), and [Scottish people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_people)

### Population



Scotland population [cartogram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartogram). The size of councils is in proportion to their population.

During the 1820s, many Scots migrated from Scotland to countries such as [Australia](4689264.md), the [United States](3434750.md) and [Canada](5042916.md), principally from the Highlands which remained poor in comparison to elsewhere in Scotland.[^cite_note-341] The Highlands was the only part of mainland Britain with a recurrent famine.[^cite_note-342] A small range of products were exported from the region, which had negligible industrial production and a continued population growth that tested the subsistence agriculture. These problems, and the desire to improve agriculture and profits were the driving forces of the ongoing [Highland Clearances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_Clearances), in which many of the population of the Highlands suffered eviction as lands were enclosed, principally so that they could be used for sheep farming. The first phase of the clearances followed patterns of agricultural change throughout Britain. The second phase was driven by overpopulation, the [Highland Potato Famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_Potato_Famine) and the collapse of industries that had relied on the wartime economy of the Napoleonic Wars.[^cite_note-343]

The population of Scotland grew steadily in the 19th century, from 1,608,000 in the census of 1801 to 2,889,000 in 1851 and 4,472,000 in 1901.[^cite_note-344] Even with the development of industry, there were not enough good jobs. As a result, during the period 1841–1931, about 2 million Scots migrated to North America and Australia, and another 750,000 Scots relocated to England.[^cite_note-Huston\&Knox2001pxxxii-345] Caused by the advent of [refrigeration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refrigeration) and imports of lamb, mutton and wool from overseas, the 1870s brought with them a collapse of sheep prices and an abrupt halt in the previous sheep farming boom.[^cite_note-\:0-346]

Scotland is the [most sparsely populated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_density) [country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countries_of_the_United_Kingdom) of the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348] In August 2012, the Scottish population reached an all-time high of 5.25 million people.[^cite_note-thecourier1-349] The reasons given were that, in Scotland, births were outnumbering the number of deaths, and immigrants were moving to Scotland from overseas. In 2011, 43,700 people moved from Wales, Northern Ireland or England to live in Scotland.[^cite_note-thecourier1-349] The [most recent census in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Kingdom_census#2022_Census_for_Scotland) was conducted by the Scottish Government and the [National Records of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Records_of_Scotland) in March 2022.[^cite_note-auto2-350] The population of Scotland at the 2022 Census was 5,436,600, the highest ever,[^cite_note-auto2-350] beating the previous record of 5,295,400 at the 2011 Census. It was 5,062,011 at the 2001 Census.[^cite_note-autogenerated1-351] An ONS estimate for 2025 was 5,545,500.[^cite_note-ONS_mid-year_pop_est-8] In the 2011 Census, 62% of Scotland's population stated their [national identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_identity) as '[Scottish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_people) only', 18% as 'Scottish and British', 8% as 'British only', and 4% chose 'other identity only'.[^cite_note-352]

Throughout its history, Scotland has long had a tradition of migration from Scotland and immigration into Scotland. In 2021 the Scottish Government released figures showing that an estimated 41,000 people had immigrated from other international countries into Scotland, while an average of 22,100 people had migrated from Scotland. Scottish Government data from 2002 shows that by 2021, there had been a sharp increase in immigration to Scotland, with 2002 estimates standing at 27,800 immigrants. While immigration had increased from 2002, migration from Scotland had dropped, with 2002 estimates standing at 26,200 people migrating from Scotland.[^cite_note-353]

### Urbanisation

See also: [Cities of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cities_of_Scotland) and [List of towns and cities in Scotland by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_towns_and_cities_in_Scotland_by_population)

Although [Edinburgh](9602.md) is the capital of Scotland, the largest city is Glasgow, which has just over 584,000 inhabitants. The [Greater Glasgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Glasgow) conurbation, with a population of almost 1.2 million, is home to nearly a quarter of Scotland's population.[^cite_note-354] The [Central Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Belt) is where most of the main towns and cities of Scotland are located, including Glasgow, Edinburgh, Dundee, and Perth. Scotland's only major city outside the Central Belt is Aberdeen. The [Scottish Lowlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Lowlands) host 80% of the total population, where the Central Belt accounts for 3.5 million people.

In general, only the more accessible and larger islands remain inhabited. Currently, fewer than 90 remain inhabited. The Southern Uplands is essentially rural and dominated by agriculture and forestry.[^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356] Because of housing problems in Glasgow and Edinburgh, [five new towns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_towns_in_the_United_Kingdom#Scotland) were designated between 1947 and 1966. They are [East Kilbride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Kilbride), [Glenrothes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glenrothes), [Cumbernauld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumbernauld), [Livingston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livingston,_West_Lothian), and [Irvine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irvine,_North_Ayrshire).[^cite_note-357]

The largest council area by population is [Glasgow City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasgow_City_Council), with [Highland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_\(council_area\)) being the largest in terms of geographical area.
