### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Scotland)



The [Anglo-Scottish border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Scottish_border), welcoming travellers in both English and [Scottish Gaelic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic)

Scotland has four [indigenous languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_language): English, [Scots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_language),[^cite_note-359] [Scottish Gaelic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic),[^cite_note-360] and [British Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Sign_Language).[^cite_note-361] Scottish Standard English, a variety of English as spoken in Scotland, is at one end of a bipolar [linguistic continuum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linguistic_continuum), with broad Scots at the other.[^cite_note-362] Scottish Standard English may have been influenced to varying degrees by Scots.[^cite_note-Stuart-Smith_J._2008._p.48-363][^cite_note-364] [Highland English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_English) is spoken in that region, while Gaelic is mostly spoken in the [Western Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Isles), where it continues to be used by a large proportion of residents.

Overall, the use of Scotland's indigenous languages other than English has declined since the 19th century. The 2011 census indicated that 63% of the population had "no skills in Scots".[^cite_note-2022_census-358] The use of Gaelic is confined to 1% of the population.[^cite_note-365] The number of Gaelic speakers in Scotland dropped from 250,000 in 1881 to 60,000 in 2008.[^cite_note-366] Across the whole of Scotland, the 2011 census showed that 25,000 people (0.49% of the population) used Gaelic at home.[^cite_note-367] In the 21st century, moves were made to provide official support to these languages, beginning with formal recognition as minority languages and culminating in both Scots and Gaelic being given official status in Scotland in 2025.[^cite_note-368]

The most common language spoken at home in Scotland after English and Scots is Polish, with about 1.1% of the population, or 54,000 people.[^cite_note-369] Immigration since World War II has given Glasgow, Edinburgh, and Dundee small South Asian communities.[^cite_note-370] In 2011 there were an estimated 49,000 ethnically Pakistani people living in Scotland, making them the largest non-White ethnic group.[^cite_note-ethnicity-371] The 2004 [enlargement of the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlargement_of_the_European_Union) spurred an increase in migration from [Central](5188.md) and [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe) to Scotland, and the 2011 census indicated that 61,000 [Poles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_people) lived there.[^cite_note-ethnicity-371][^cite_note-372]

There are many more people with Scottish ancestry living abroad than the total population of Scotland. In the 2000 Census, 9.2 million Americans self-reported some degree of [Scottish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_American) descent.[^cite_note-373] [Ulster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulster)'s Protestant population is mainly of Lowland Scottish descent,[^cite_note-374] and it is estimated that there are more than 27 million descendants of the [Scots-Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulster_Scots_people) migration now living in the US.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376] In Canada, the [Scottish-Canadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish-Canadian) community accounts for 4.7 million people.[^cite_note-cancensus-377] About 20% of the original European settler population of New Zealand came from Scotland.[^cite_note-378]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Scotland)



[St Mary's Cathedral, Edinburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Mary's_Cathedral,_Edinburgh_\(Episcopal\))

In the 2022 Census, a majority of Scots (51.12%) reported not following any religion. The most practised religion is Christianity (38.79%), mostly the [Church of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Scotland) (20.36%) and [Roman Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Scotland) (13.3%).[^cite_note-\:1-379] In almost every council area, the most common response to the census question was "No religion", except in [Na h-Eileanan Siar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_Hebrides) and [Inverclyde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inverclyde), where the Church of Scotland (35.3%) and Catholicism (33.4%) were the most common responses, respectively.[^cite_note-\:1-379]

Forms of [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Christianity_in_Scotland) have dominated religious life in what is now Scotland for more than 1,400 years.[^cite_note-380][^cite_note-381] Since the [Scottish Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Reformation) of 1560, the [national church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_church) (the [Church of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Scotland), also known as [The Kirk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirk_\(word\))) has been [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) in orientation and [Reformed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformed_theology) in theology. Since 1689 it has had a [Presbyterian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterian) system of church government independent from the state.[^cite_note-Keay-21] Its membership dropped just below 300,000 in 2020 (5% of the total population)[^cite_note-382][^cite_note-scotsman.com-383][^cite_note-384] The Church operates a territorial parish structure, with every community in Scotland having a local congregation.

Scotland also has a significant [Roman Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church_in_Scotland) population with 13.3% professing that faith, particularly in Greater Glasgow and the north-west.[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-\:1-379] After the Reformation, Roman Catholicism in Scotland continued in the Highlands and some western islands like [Uist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uist) and [Barra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra), and it was strengthened during the 19th century by immigration from Ireland. Other Christian denominations in Scotland include the [Free Church of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Church_of_Scotland_\(since_1900\)), and various other Presbyterian offshoots. Scotland's third largest church is the [Scottish Episcopal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Episcopal_Church).[^cite_note-386]

Other minority faiths include [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Scotland) (2.2%), [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Scotland) (0.55%), Sikhism, Buddhism and Judaism.[^cite_note-\:1-379][^cite_note-2011_census_religion-387][^cite_note-GROSCOT-388] In the 2022 census, 10,988 people identified their religion as Sikh, 15,501 as Buddhist and 5,847 as Jewish.[^cite_note-389] The Gudwara Guru Granth Sahib Sikh Sabha in [Pollkshields, Glasgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pollokshields), which opened in 2013, is Scotland's first purpose-built Sikh Gudwara.[^cite_note-390] The [Samyé Ling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samy%C3%A9_Ling) monastery near [Eskdalemuir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eskdalemuir), which celebrated its 40th anniversary in 2007, is the oldest [Buddhist](3267529.md) monastery in Western Europe.[^cite_note-391] Glasgow's [Garnethill Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garnethill_Synagogue), opened in 1879, is the oldest surviving synagogue in Scotland and today houses the [Scottish Jewish Archives Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Jewish_Archives_Centre). Jewish communities have existed in Scotland since the late 17th century, with organised congregations established in Edinburgh and Glasgow in the early 19th century.[^cite_note-392][^cite_note-393]

### Education

Main article: [Education in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Scotland)



Founded in 1413, the [University of St. Andrews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_St._Andrews) is the oldest in Scotland and one of the oldest worldwide

[^cite_note-394]

The [Scottish education system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Scotland) has always had a characteristic emphasis on a [broad education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_education).[^cite_note-395] In the 15th century, the [Humanist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanism) emphasis on education culminated with the passing of the [Education Act 1496](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_Act_1496), which decreed that all sons of barons and freeholders of substance should attend grammar schools to learn "perfyct Latyne", resulting in an increase in literacy among a male and wealthy elite.[^cite_note-Bawcutt\&Williams2006pp29-30-396] In the Reformation, the 1560 *[First Book of Discipline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Book_of_Discipline)* set out a plan for a school in every parish, but this proved financially impossible.[^cite_note-397] In 1616 an [act in Privy Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_Establishment_Act_1616) required every parish to establish a school.[^cite_note-398] By the late 17th century there was a largely complete network of parish schools in the Lowlands, but in the Highlands basic education was still lacking in many areas.[^cite_note-Anderson2003-399] Education remained a matter for the church rather than the state until the [Education (Scotland) Act 1872](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_\(Scotland\)_Act_1872).[^cite_note-400]

Policy and decision making relating to education in Scotland is the responsibility of the [Scottish Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Government), and is overseen by executive agencies [Education Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_Scotland) (responsible for quality improvement) and [His Majesty's Inspectorate of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/His_Majesty's_Inspectorate_of_Education) (responsible for school inspections).[^cite_note-401] The *[Curriculum for Excellence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curriculum_for_Excellence)*, Scotland's national school curriculum, presently provides the curricular framework for children and young people from age 3 to 18.[^cite_note-402] All 3- and 4-year-old children in Scotland are entitled to a free nursery place. Formal primary education begins at about 5 years old and lasts for 7 years (P1–P7); children in Scotland study National Qualifications of the Curriculum for Excellence between the ages of 14 and 18. The school leaving age is 16, after which students may choose to remain at school and study for further qualifications. A small number of students at certain [private schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_private_schools_in_Scotland) may follow the [English system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_England) and study towards [GCSEs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GCSE) and [A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_Level_\(UK\)) and AS-Levels instead.[^cite_note-403]

There are fifteen [Scottish universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_universities), some of which are among the [oldest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_oldest_universities_in_continuous_operation).[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405] The four universities founded before the end of the 16th century – the universities of [St Andrews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_St_Andrews), [Glasgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Glasgow), [Aberdeen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Aberdeen) and [Edinburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Edinburgh) – are collectively known as the [ancient universities of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_universities_of_Scotland). They all rank among the 200 best universities in the world in the [THE rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Times_Higher_Education_World_University_Rankings), with Edinburgh in the top 50.[^cite_note-406] Scotland had more universities per capita in [QS' World University Rankings'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QS_World_University_Rankings) top 100 in 2012 than any other nation.[^cite_note-407] The country produces 1% of the world's [published research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_publishing) with less than 0.1% of the world's population, and higher education institutions account for 9% of Scotland's service sector exports.[^cite_note-408][^cite_note-409] Scotland's University Courts are the only bodies in Scotland authorised to award degrees.

### Health

Main articles: [Health in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Scotland) and [Healthcare in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Scotland)

Further information: [List of hospitals in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_Scotland) and [Social care in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_care_in_Scotland)



[NHS Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHS_Scotland)'s [Queen Elizabeth University Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_University_Hospital), Glasgow. It is the largest hospital campus in Europe.

[^cite_note-410]

Health care in Scotland is mainly provided by [NHS Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHS_Scotland), Scotland's public health care system. This was founded by the [National Health Service (Scotland) Act 1947](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Service_\(Scotland\)_Act_1947) (later repealed by the National Health Service (Scotland) Act 1978) that took effect on 5 July 1948 to coincide with the launch of the NHS in England and Wales. Prior to 1948, half of Scotland's landmass was already covered by state-funded health care, provided by the [Highlands and Islands Medical Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highlands_and_Islands_Medical_Service).[^cite_note-411] Healthcare policy and funding is the responsibility of the Scottish Government's [Health Directorates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Government_Health_Directorates). In 2014, the NHS in Scotland had around 140,000 staff.[^cite_note-412]

The [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) (TFR) in Scotland is below the replacement rate of 2.1 (the TFR was 1.73 in 2011[^cite_note-413]). The majority of births are to unmarried women (51.3% of births were outside of marriage in 2012[^cite_note-414]).

Life expectancy between 2012 and 2014 was 77.1 years for males and 81.1 years for females.[^cite_note-life_expectancy_2015-415] This is the lowest of any of the four countries of the UK.[^cite_note-life_expectancy_2015-415] The number of hospital admissions in Scotland for diseases such as cancer was 2,528 in 2002. Over the next ten years, by 2012, this had increased to 2,669.[^cite_note-hospitaladmissions-416] Hospital admissions for other diseases, such as coronary heart disease (CHD) were lower, with 727 admissions in 2002, and decreasing to 489 in 2012.[^cite_note-hospitaladmissions-416]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Scotland)

### World Heritage Sites

Main article: [List of World Heritage Sites in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Scotland)



[New Lanark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Lanark), one of the UNESCO World Heritage Sites in Scotland

Scotland has seven [World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Scotland) – [St. Kilda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Kilda,_Scotland), [Edinburgh Old Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edinburgh_Old_Town) and [New Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edinburgh_New_Town), The [Heart of Neolithic Orkney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heart_of_Neolithic_Orkney), [New Lanark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Lanark), The [Antonine Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonine_Wall), The [Forth Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forth_Bridge) and The [Flow Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flow_Country).[^cite_note-417] [Zenith of Iron Age Shetland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zenith_of_Iron_Age_Shetland) is currently a candidate for World Heritage Site status from [UNESCO](21786641.md).[^cite_note-418] [Historic Environment Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_Environment_Scotland) is responsible for 'cultural' sites as part of their wider responsibility towards the historic environment. The [Environment Directorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environment_Directorate) is responsible for natural sites.[^cite_note-419]

### Celtic connections

Further information: [Celtic nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_nations)

As one of the [Celtic nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_nations), Scotland and Scottish culture are represented at inter-Celtic events at home and over the world. Scotland hosts several music festivals including [Celtic Connections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_Connections) (Glasgow), and the [Hebridean Celtic Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebridean_Celtic_Festival) (Stornoway). Festivals celebrating Celtic culture, such as [Festival Interceltique de Lorient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festival_Interceltique_de_Lorient) ([Brittany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brittany)), the [Pan Celtic Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_Celtic_Festival) (Ireland), and the National Celtic Festival ([Portarlington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portarlington,_Victoria), Australia), feature elements of Scottish culture such as language, music and dance.[^cite_note-Celtic_connections_1-420][^cite_note-Festival_1-421][^cite_note-Dingle_1-422][^cite_note-Festival_3-423]

### Literature, drama and media

Main articles: [Scottish literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_literature), [Theatre of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_Scotland), [Media of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Scotland), [Scottish art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_art), [Comedy in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comedy_in_Scotland), [Festivals in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festivals_in_Scotland), and [Cinema of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Scotland)



World renowned poet [Robert Burns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Burns) is considered the national poet, best known for works such as "[Auld Lang Syne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auld_Lang_Syne)" and writing in the Scots language

Scotland has a literary heritage dating back to the early Middle Ages. The earliest extant literature composed in what is now Scotland was in [Brythonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brythonic_languages) speech in the 6th century, but is preserved as part of [Welsh literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh-language_literature).[^cite_note-424] Later medieval literature included works in Latin,[^cite_note-425] Gaelic,[^cite_note-426] Old English[^cite_note-427] and French.[^cite_note-428] The first surviving major text in [Early Scots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Scots) is the 14th-century poet [John Barbour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Barbour_\(poet\))'s epic *[Brus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Brus)*, focusing on the life of Robert I,[^cite_note-429] and was soon followed by a series of vernacular romances and prose works.[^cite_note-Wormald1991pp60-7-430] In the 16th century, the crown's patronage helped the development of Scots drama and poetry,[^cite_note-Brownetalpp256-7-431] but the accession of James VI to the English throne removed a major centre of literary patronage and Scots was sidelined as a literary language.[^cite_note-432] Interest in Scots literature was revived in the 18th century by figures including [James Macpherson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Macpherson), whose [Ossian Cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ossian) made him the first Scottish poet to gain an international reputation and was a major influence on the European Enlightenment.[^cite_note-433] It was also a major influence on [Robert Burns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Burns), whom many consider the national poet,[^cite_note-434] and [Walter Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Scott), whose [Waverley Novels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waverley_Novels) did much to define Scottish identity in the 19th century.[^cite_note-435] Towards the end of the Victorian era a number of Scottish-born authors achieved international reputations as writers in English, including [Robert Louis Stevenson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Louis_Stevenson), [Arthur Conan Doyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Conan_Doyle), [J. M. Barrie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._M._Barrie) and [George MacDonald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_MacDonald).[^cite_note-Cultural_Profile-92]

In the 20th century the [Scottish Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Renaissance) saw a surge of literary activity and attempts to reclaim the Scots language as a medium for serious literature.[^cite_note-VisitingArtsScotland-436] Members of the movement were followed by a new generation of post-war poets including [Edwin Morgan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwin_Morgan_\(poet\)), who would be appointed the first Makar by the Scottish Government in 2004.[^cite_note-437] [Sorley MacLean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorley_MacLean) was described by the [Scottish Poetry Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Poetry_Library) as "one of the major Scottish poets of the modern era" because of his "mastery of his chosen medium and his engagement with the European poetic tradition and European politics".[^cite_note-library-438] Nobel Prize Laureate [Seamus Heaney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seamus_Heaney) credited MacLean with saving [Scottish Gaelic poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic_literature).[^cite_note-439] From the 1980s Scottish literature enjoyed another major revival, particularly associated with a group of writers including [Irvine Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irvine_Welsh).[^cite_note-VisitingArtsScotland-436] Scottish poets who emerged in the same period included [Carol Ann Duffy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carol_Ann_Duffy), who, in May 2009, was the first Scot named the monarch's [Poet Laureate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poet_Laureate).[^cite_note-440] Scotland's national poet is known as the [Makar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makar) and is appointed by the first minister to promote literacy, writing and poetry across the country, as well as to produce work for national events and produce annual reports to the government.[^cite_note-441] The incumbent Makar is [Pàdraig MacAoidh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A0draig_MacAoidh), the first Scottish Gaelic writer to hold the position.[^cite_note-442]

National newspapers such as the [*Daily Record*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daily_Record_\(Scotland\)), [*The Herald*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Herald_\(Glasgow\)), *[The Scotsman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Scotsman)* and *[The National](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_National_\(Scotland\))* are all produced in Scotland.[^cite_note-Newspapers-443] Important regional dailies include the [Evening News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edinburgh_Evening_News) in Edinburgh, *[The Courier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Courier_\(Dundee\))* in Dundee in the east, and *[The Press and Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Press_and_Journal)* serving Aberdeen and the north.[^cite_note-Newspapers-443] Scotland is represented at the [Celtic Media Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_Media_Festival), which showcases film and television from the Celtic countries. Scottish entrants have won many awards since the festival began in 1980.[^cite_note-Media_1-444] Scotland's national broadcaster is [BBC Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Scotland), a division of the [BBC](19344654.md), which runs three national [television stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_television_stations), [BBC One Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_One_Scotland), [BBC Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Scotland_\(TV_channel\)) and the Gaelic-language [BBC Alba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Alba), and the national radio stations, *[BBC Radio Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Radio_Scotland)* and *[BBC Radio nan Gàidheal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Radio_nan_G%C3%A0idheal)*. The main Scottish commercial television station is [STV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STV_\(TV_network\)) which broadcasts on two of the three [ITV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITV_\(network\)) regions of Scotland.[^cite_note-445] Scotland's [film industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Scotland) is supported by [Screen Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creative_Scotland), the [executive non-departmental public body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-departmental_public_body) of the Scottish Government which provides funding and support for film production in the country.[^cite_note-446]

### Music

Main article: [Music of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Scotland)



The [bagpipes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagpipes) are an instrument largely associated with Scotland, and an early example of popular Scottish music

[Scottish music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_music) is a significant aspect of the nation's culture, with both traditional and modern influences. A famous traditional Scottish instrument is the [Great Highland bagpipe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Highland_bagpipe), a woodwind reed instrument consisting of three drones and a melody pipe (called the chanter), which are fed continuously by a reservoir of air in a bag. The popularity of [pipe bands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pipe_band)—primarily featuring bagpipes, various types of snares and drums, and showcasing Scottish traditional dress and music—has spread throughout the world. Bagpipes are featured in holiday celebrations, parades, funerals, weddings, and other events internationally. Many military regiments have a pipe band of their own. In addition to the Great Highland pipes, several smaller, somewhat quieter [bellows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellows)-blown varieties of bagpipe are played in Scotland, including the [smallpipes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_smallpipes) and the [Border pipes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_pipes).

[Scottish popular music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Scotland#Pop,_rock_and_fusion) has gained an international following, with artists such as [Lewis Capaldi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_Capaldi), [Amy Macdonald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Macdonald), [KT Tunstall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KT_Tunstall), [Nina Nesbitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nina_Nesbitt), [Chvrches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chvrches), [Gerry Cinnamon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerry_Cinnamon) and [Paolo Nutini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paolo_Nutini) gaining international success. DJ [Calvin Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvin_Harris) was one of the [most streamed artists on Spotify](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most-streamed_artists_on_Spotify) in 2023,[^cite_note-447][^cite_note-448] whilst [Susan Boyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_Boyle)'s [debut album](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_Dreamed_a_Dream_\(album\)) was [one of the best-selling albums of the 21st century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_best-selling_albums_of_the_21st_century), and was the best-selling album internationally in 2009.[^cite_note-449] [Runrig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Runrig) popularised [Celtic rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_rock) and frequently performed in [Scottish Gaelic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic), with their 1995 single "[An Ubhal as Àirde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An_Ubhal_as_%C3%80irde_\(The_Highest_Apple\))" the first song in the language to reach the UK Top 20.[^cite_note-450] Musical talent in Scotland is recognised via the [Scottish Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Music_Awards), [Scottish Album of the Year Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Album_of_the_Year_Award), the [Scots Trad Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_Trad_Music_Awards) and the [BBC Radio Scotland Young Traditional Musician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Radio_Scotland_Young_Traditional_Musician) award.

### Cuisine

Main article: [Scottish cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_cuisine)



[Haggis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haggis), [neeps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutabaga) and [tatties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potato)

Scottish cuisine has distinctive attributes and recipes of its own but shares much with wider [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_cuisine) and [European cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_cuisine) as a result of local and foreign influences, both ancient and modern. Traditional Scottish dishes exist alongside international foodstuffs brought about by immigration. Scotland's natural larder of [game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Game_\(food\)), dairy products, fish, fruit, and vegetables is the chief factor in traditional Scots cooking, with a high reliance on simplicity and a lack of [spices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spice) from abroad, as these were historically rare and expensive.[^cite_note-451]

[Irn-Bru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irn-Bru) is the most common Scottish carbonated soft drink, often described as "Scotland's other national drink" (after whisky).[^cite_note-452] During the [Late Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Middle_Ages) and [early modern era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_era), [French cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_cuisine) played a role in Scottish cookery due to cultural exchanges brought about by the "[Auld Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auld_Alliance)",[^cite_note-453] especially during the reign of [Mary, Queen of Scots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary,_Queen_of_Scots). Mary, on her return to Scotland, brought an entourage of French staff who are considered responsible for revolutionising Scots cooking and for some of Scotland's unique food terminology.[^cite_note-454]

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Scotland)



The [national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_national_football_team) line up ahead of a game with [Bolivia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivia_national_football_team), June 2026

Scotland hosts its own national sporting competitions and has independent representation at several international sporting events, including the [FIFA World Cup](11370.md), the [UEFA Nations League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Nations_League), the [UEFA European Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_European_Championship), the [Rugby World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_World_Cup), the [Rugby League World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_League_World_Cup), the [Cricket World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cricket_World_Cup), the [Netball World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netball_World_Cup) and the [Commonwealth Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Games). Scotland has its own national governing bodies, such as the [Scottish Football Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Football_Association) (the second oldest national football association in the world)[^cite_note-455] and the [Scottish Rugby Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Rugby_Union). Variations of football have been played in Scotland for centuries, with the earliest reference dating back to 1424.[^cite_note-FIFA-456]

The world's first official international association football match, between [Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_national_football_team) and [England](9904.md) was held in Glasgow on 30 November 1872, and resulted in a 0–0 draw.[^cite_note-457] The [Scottish Cup](883238.md) was first contested in 1873, and is the oldest trophy in association football.[^cite_note-458] The [Scottish Football Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Football_Association) (SFA) is the main [governing body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_governing_body) for Scottish association football, and a founding member of the [International Football Association Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Football_Association_Board) (IFAB) which governs the [Laws of the Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laws_of_the_Game_\(association_football\)). Scotland is one of only four countries to have a permanent representative on the IFAB; the other four representatives being appointed for set periods by [FIFA](11049.md).[^cite_note-459][^cite_note-460] The SFA has responsibility for the Scotland national football team and the [Scotland women's team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_women's_national_football_team).



The [Old Course at St Andrews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Course_at_St_Andrews), the oldest [golf](19568112.md) course in the world

With the modern game of [golf](19568112.md) originating in 15th-century Scotland, the country is promoted as the [home of golf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golf_in_Scotland).[^cite_note-461][^cite_note-462][^cite_note-463] To many golfers the [Old Course](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Course_at_St_Andrews) in the Fife town of [St Andrews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Andrews), an ancient [links](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Links_\(golf\)) course dating to before 1552,[^cite_note-464] is considered a site of pilgrimage.[^cite_note-465] In 1764, the standard 18-hole golf course was created at St Andrews when members modified the course from 22 to 18 holes.[^cite_note-466] The world's oldest golf tournament, and golf's first major, is [The Open Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Open_Championship), which was first played on 17 October 1860 at Prestwick Golf Club, in Ayrshire, Scotland, with [Scottish golfers winning the earliest majors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Men's_major_golf_championships#Major_championship_winners).[^cite_note-467] There are many other famous golf courses in Scotland, including [Carnoustie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnoustie_Golf_Links), [Gleneagles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gleneagles_\(Scotland\)), [Muirfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muirfield), and [Royal Troon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Troon).

The Scottish Rugby Union is the second oldest rugby union in the world. [Murrayfield Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murrayfield_Stadium) in Edinburgh is the national stadium of the [Scottish national rugby team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_national_rugby_union_team). The Scotland rugby team played [their first official](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1871_Scotland_versus_England_rugby_union_match) [test match](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Test_match_\(rugby_union\)), winning 1–0 against England at [Raeburn Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raeburn_Place) in 1871. Scotland has competed in the [Six Nations](26547.md) from the inaugural tournament in 1883, winning it 14 times outright—including the last Five Nations in [1999](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Five_Nations_Championship)—and sharing it another 8 times. The Rugby World Cup was introduced in 1987 and Scotland have competed in all nine competitions, the most recent being in the [2023 Rugby World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Rugby_World_Cup). Scotland competes with the England rugby team annually for the [Calcutta Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcutta_Cup). Each year, this fixture is played out as part of the Six Nations, with Scotland having last won in 2026.[^cite_note-468]



[Murrayfield Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murrayfield_Stadium), the national stadium of the [Scottish national rugby team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_national_rugby_union_team)

Other distinctive features of the national sporting culture include the [Highland games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_games), [curling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curling) and [shinty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinty). In [boxing](4243.md), Scotland has had 13 world champions, including [Ken Buchanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Buchanan), [Benny Lynch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benny_Lynch) and [Jim Watt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Watt_\(boxer\)). Scotland has also been successful in [motorsport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorsport), particularly in [Formula One](10854.md). Notable drivers include; [David Coulthard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Coulthard), [Jim Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Clark), [Paul Di Resta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Di_Resta), and [Jackie Stewart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackie_Stewart).[^cite_note-469] In [IndyCar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IndyCar_Series), [Dario Franchitti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dario_Franchitti) has won 4 consecutive IndyCar world championships.[^cite_note-470]

Scotland has competed at every [Commonwealth Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Games) since 1930 and has won 508 medals in total—134 Gold, 146 Silver and 228 Bronze.[^cite_note-471] Scotland has [hosted the Commonwealth Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Commonwealth_Games_venues) four times – Edinburgh played host to the Commonwealth Games in [1970](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_British_Commonwealth_Games) and [1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Commonwealth_Games), and [Glasgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasgow) in [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Commonwealth_Games) and [2026](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Commonwealth_Games).[^cite_note-472] Glasgow was confirmed as the host city for the [2026 Commonwealth Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Commonwealth_Games) in September 2024.[^cite_note-473] Edinburgh was the host city for the inaugural [Commonwealth Youth Games in 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Commonwealth_Youth_Games).[^cite_note-474]

### National identity

Main article: [Scottish national identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_national_identity)

Further information: [Scottish people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_people) and [National symbols of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_Scotland)

Royal Arms of Scotland

The [Royal Arms of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Arms_of_Scotland)

Scottish Thistle

The [spear thistle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spear_thistle), the [national emblem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_emblem) of Scotland

The image of [St. Andrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Andrew), martyred while bound to an X-shaped cross, first appeared in the [Kingdom of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland) during the reign of [William I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_the_Lion).[^cite_note-NAS-475] Following the death of [King Alexander III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_III_of_Scotland) in 1286 an image of Andrew was used on the [seal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seal_\(emblem\)) of the [Guardians of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guardians_of_Scotland) who assumed control of the kingdom during the subsequent [interregnum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interregnum).[^cite_note-NAS-475] Use of a simplified symbol associated with Saint Andrew, the [saltire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saltire), has its origins in the late 14th century; the [Parliament of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Scotland) decreeing in 1385 that Scottish soldiers should wear a white Saint Andrew's Cross on the front and back of their tunics.[^cite_note-476] Use of a blue background for the Saint Andrew's Cross is said to date from at least the 15th century.[^cite_note-477] Since 1606 the saltire has also formed part of the design of the [Union Flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Flag).

There are numerous other symbols and symbolic artefacts, both official and unofficial, including the [thistle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thistle), the nation's [floral emblem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_emblem) (celebrated in the song, [The Thistle o' Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Thistle_o'_Scotland)), the [Declaration of Arbroath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Arbroath), incorporating a statement of political independence made on 6 April 1320, the textile pattern [tartan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tartan) that often signifies a particular [Scottish clan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_clan) and the royal [Lion Rampant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Standard_of_Scotland) flag.[^cite_note-478][^cite_note-479][^cite_note-480] The [Dress Act 1746](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dress_Act_1746), which, prohibited the wearing of tartans, was repealed in 1782 after a campaign led by [James Graham, 3rd Duke of Montrose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Graham,_3rd_Duke_of_Montrose).[^cite_note-Works-481]

Scotland has its own [regalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regalia) known as the [Honours of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honours_of_Scotland) (informally the [Scottish Crown Jewels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Crown_Jewels)) which consists of the [Crown of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_Scotland), a Sceptre and [Scottish Sword of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Sword_of_State). The Scottish crown was worn by [Scottish monarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_monarchs) during their coronation, and today is kept in the Crown Room at [Edinburgh Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edinburgh_Castle). The Crown of Scotland is present at each state opening of the Scottish Parliament.[^cite_note-482] Collectively, the Honours of Scotland are the oldest regalia in the [British Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Isles), dating from the late 15th and early 16th centuries.[^cite_note-483]



The [Crown of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_Scotland)

Although there is no official [national anthem of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_anthem_of_Scotland),[^cite_note-484] *[Flower of Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flower_of_Scotland)* is played on special occasions and sporting events such as football and rugby matches involving the Scotland national teams and since 2010 is also played at the Commonwealth Games after it was voted the overwhelming favourite by participating Scottish athletes.[^cite_note-485] Other currently less popular candidates for the National Anthem of Scotland include *[Scotland the Brave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland_the_Brave)*, *[Highland Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_Cathedral)*, *[Scots Wha Hae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_Wha_Hae)* and *[A Man's A Man for A' That](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Man's_A_Man_for_A'_That)*.[^cite_note-486]

[St Andrew's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Andrew's_Day), 30 November, is the [national day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_day), although [Burns' Night](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burns'_Night) tends to be more widely observed, particularly outside Scotland. In 2006, the Scottish Parliament passed the [St Andrew's Day Bank Holiday (Scotland) Act 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Andrew's_Day_Bank_Holiday_\(Scotland\)_Act_2007), designating the day an official [bank holiday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_holiday).[^cite_note-487] [Tartan Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tartan_Day) is a recent innovation from Canada.[^cite_note-488]

The national animal of Scotland is the [unicorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unicorn), which has been a Scottish heraldic symbol since the 12th century.[^cite_note-489] The [Court of the Lord Lyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_the_Lord_Lyon) regulates [Scottish heraldry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_heraldry) and the Public Register of All Armorial Bearings in Scotland.[^cite_note-490]

[^cite_note-MaMu22-219]: &#x20;Masterman, Roger; Murray, Colin (2022). "The United Kingdom's Devolution Arrangements". [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1017/9781009158497](https://doi.org/10.1017%2F9781009158497). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20240504011056/https://www.cambridge.org/highereducation/books/constitutional-and-administrative-law/E1EB9FEDC43459C991E42BD945231DB5) from the original on 4 May 2024. Retrieved 27 October 2023. UK Internal Market Act 2020 imposed new restrictions on the ability of the devolved institutions to enact measures...mutual recognition and non-discrimination requirements mean that standards set by the legislatures in Wales and Scotland cannot restrict the sale of goods which are acceptable in other parts of the UK. In other words, imposing such measures would simply create competitive disadvantages for businesses in Wales and Scotland; they would not change the product standards or environmental protections applicable to all goods which can be purchased in Wales and Scotland.

[^cite_note-DouHu22-220]: &#x20;[Dougan, Michael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Dougan); Hunt, Jo; [McEwen, Nicola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicola_McEwen); McHarg, Aileen (2022). ["Sleeping with an Elephant: Devolution and the United Kingdom Internal Market Act 2020"](https://dro.dur.ac.uk/35167/). *[Law Quarterly Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_Quarterly_Review)*. **138** (Oct). London: [Sweet & Maxwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweet_&_Maxwell): 650–676. [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [0023-933X](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/0023-933X). [SSRN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SSRN_\(identifier\)) [4018581](https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=4018581). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220802185022/https://dro.dur.ac.uk/35167/) from the original on 2 August 2022. Retrieved 4 March 2022 – via [Durham Research Online](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durham_University). The Act has restrictive – and potentially damaging – consequences for the regulatory capacity of the devolved legislatures...the primary purpose of the legislation was to constrain the capacity of the devolved institutions to use their regulatory autonomy...in practice, it constrains the ability of the devolved institutions to make effective regulatory choices for their territories in ways that do not apply to the choices made by the UK government and parliament for the English market.

[^cite_note-Gu23-221]: &#x20;Guderjan, Marius (2023). [*Intergovernmental Relations in the UK: Cooperation and Conflict in a Devolved Unitary State*](https://www.routledge.com/Intergovernmental-Relations-in-the-UK-Cooperation-and-Conflict-in-a-Devolved/Guderjan/p/book/9781032394855). London/New York: [Routledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Routledge). pp. 166–176. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.4324/9781003349952](https://doi.org/10.4324%2F9781003349952). [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-032-39485-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-032-39485-5). [S2CID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S2CID_\(identifier\)) [257877108](https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID\:257877108). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20240701053606/https://www.routledge.com/Intergovernmental-Relations-in-the-UK-Cooperation-and-Conflict-in-a-Devolved/Guderjan/p/book/9781032394855) from the original on 1 July 2024. Retrieved 4 November 2023. Since the act became law on 17 December 2020, the devolved administrations can continue to set standards for goods and services produced within their territory, but their rules do not apply to goods and services coming from other jurisdictions. They also must accept products imported into one part of the UK. This undermines their legislative autonomy and renders certain policies ineffective

[^cite_note-Keating21-222]: &#x20;[Keating, Michael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Keating_\(political_scientist\)) (2 February 2021). ["Taking back control? Brexit and the territorial constitution of the United Kingdom"](https://doi.org/10.1080%2F13501763.2021.1876156). *[Journal of European Public Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_European_Public_Policy)*. **29** (4). Abingdon: [Taylor & Francis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taylor_&_Francis): 491–509. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1080/13501763.2021.1876156](https://doi.org/10.1080%2F13501763.2021.1876156). [hdl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hdl_\(identifier\)):[1814/70296](https://hdl.handle.net/1814%2F70296). The UK Internal Market Act gives ministers sweeping powers to enforce mutual recognition and non-discrimination across the four jurisdictions. Existing differences and some social and health matters are exempted but these are much less extensive than the exemptions permitted under the EU Internal Market provisions. Only after an amendment in the House of Lords, the Bill was amended to provide a weak and non-binding consent mechanism for amendments (equivalent to the Sewel Convention) to the list of exemptions. The result is that, while the devolved governments retain regulatory competences, these are undermined by the fact that goods and services originating in, or imported into, England can be marketed anywhere.

[^cite_note-223]: &#x20;Lydgate, Emily; Anthony, Chloe (September 2022). ["Brexit, food law and the UK's search for a post-EU identity"](https://doi.org/10.1111%2F1468-2230.12735). *[Modern Law Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Law_Review)*. **85** (5). London: [Wiley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wiley_\(publisher\)): 1168–1190. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1111/1468-2230.12735](https://doi.org/10.1111%2F1468-2230.12735). While the mutual recognition principle preserves devolved powers, rather than requiring that devolved nations conform with a wide range of harmonised standards (as they did in the EU), the Act undermines devolution simply because devolved legislation will no longer apply to all relevant activity in the devolved territory...Devolution is also undermined by the asymmetry of legislative authority...the UK Internal Market Act is a protected enactment, which devolved administrations are unable to appeal or modify, but which the UK parliament will be able to modify when legislating for England.

[^cite_note-DouganMcEwen20-224]: &#x20;[Dougan, Michael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Dougan); [Hayward, Katy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katy_Hayward); Hunt, Jo; [McEwen, Nicola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicola_McEwen); McHarg, Aileen; [Wincott, Daniel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Wincott) (2020). [UK and the Internal Market, Devolution and the Union](https://www.centreonconstitutionalchange.ac.uk/publications/uk-and-internal-market-devolution-and-union). Centre on Constitutional Change (Report). [University of Edinburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Edinburgh); [University of Aberdeen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Aberdeen). pp. 2–3. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20201018185830/https://www.centreonconstitutionalchange.ac.uk/publications/uk-and-internal-market-devolution-and-union) from the original on 18 October 2020. Retrieved 16 October 2020. The market access principles undermine devolved competences in two ways...\[they] significantly undermine the purpose of devolution, which was to enable the devolved nations and regions to legislate according to their own local needs and political preferences.

[^cite_note-Arm22-225]: &#x20;Armstrong, Kenneth A. (May 2022). ["The Governance of Economic Unionism after the United Kingdom Internal Market Act"](https://doi.org/10.1111%2F1468-2230.12706). *[Modern Law Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Law_Review)*. **85** (3). Oxford: [Wiley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wiley_\(publisher\)): 635–660. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1111/1468-2230.12706](https://doi.org/10.1111%2F1468-2230.12706). So when used to disapply relevant requirements in a destination devolved jurisdiction the effect is different from that generated by the devolution statutes when they treat rules that are outside of competence as being 'not law'. In this way, the legislative competence of each jurisdiction is formally maintained, but its exercise constrained by the extraterritorial reach of regulatory norms applicable elsewhere in the UK and by the potential for regulatory competition where local producers are subject to local rules but competing goods can enter that market in compliance with the regulatory standards from where they originate...the UKIM Act 2020 allows extraterritorial application of rules that reflect different preferences or even undermines local preferences through regulatory competition, its effects are not insignificant for devolved legislatures.
